## Abstract

Mitochondria and lysosomes are central regulators of cellular metabolism, nutrient sensing, cellular quality control, and stress adaptation. Although traditionally studied as independent organelles, it is now evident that they operate as an integrated functional network through multiple modes of communication, including membrane contact sites, signaling pathways, and selective vesicular trafficking. Disruption of this crosstalk is increasingly recognized as a common feature of mitochondrial disorders, lysosomal storage diseases, neurodegeneration, cardiovascular disease, and aging, highlighting its importance for cellular and tissue homeostasis. While mitophagy has long been considered the principal interface between mitochondria and lysosomes, recent discoveries have revealed a much broader communication landscape in which bidirectional signaling, ion exchange, lipid transfer, and mitochondria-derived vesicles coordinate organelle function independently of bulk degradation. In this Review, we discuss the molecular mechanisms that mediate mitochondria–lysosome communication, emphasizing emerging concepts in inter-organelle signaling and membrane contact biology. We further examine how disruption of these pathways contributes to human disease and consider the growing evidence that restoring mitochondria–lysosome communication, rather than targeting either organelle in isolation, represents a promising therapeutic strategy.

## Introduction

The biology of organelles has been enticing the curiosity of scientists for almost 200 years, and more intensely in the past eight decades. The application of differential centrifugation in the 1950s unleashed a first wave of seminal papers based on reductionist approaches: isolate an organelle as pure as possible and study the biochemistry that it harbors. These approaches gave a framework to metabolic compartmentalization: we finally knew which enzymes were where, doing what. The metabolic roles eventually labeled with organelles: mitochondria make ATP, _ergo_ they are the energy factories of the cell, the powerhouses; lysosomes are acidic and digest macromolecules, _ergo_ they are the cell’s recycling center. The golden age of human genetics in the 90 s and 2000s systemically pinpointed organelle defects as primary causes of severe diseases. The advances in microscopy in the early 2000’s resulted in a shift from focusing on the metabolic roles of the organelles towards focusing on their behavior: their morphology, dynamics, positioning inside the cell, and suborganellar structure. The age of omics brought holistic perspectives: mass spectrometry provided the large-scale inventories of organelle-specific proteomes, and transcriptomics clarified how transcriptional programs coordinate organelle biogenesis. More recently, the methods for rapid organelle immunoisolation allowed the combination of holistic and reductionist approaches, and _in organello_ characterization, almost in real time. While organelle-specific research remains a very active topic encompassing the fields of cell biology, biochemistry, metabolism, and molecular medicine, new questions involving organelles have steadily gained traction and are now a major focus of organelle biology: how do organelles communicate with each other? What are the messages and what are the messengers? What coordinates the behavior of different organelles with cellular metabolism? _Inter-organelle communication_ has emerged as a fundamental organizing principle of cell biology, revealing that organelles function not as isolated biochemical compartments but as components of integrated communication networks that coordinate cellular physiology.

Here, we propose a unified framework for mitochondria–lysosome communication, through multiple complementary pathways—including membrane contacts, selective vesicular trafficking, diffusible metabolic signals, and signaling cascades—that together constitute an integrated communication network. Throughout this Review, we discuss how these communication pathways coordinate cellular homeostasis and how their progressive disruption underlies diverse human diseases. It is however important to note that while this review is focused on mitochondria–lysosome communication, the interaction between these organelles is not exclusive. Most organelles interact with others. For example, both mitochondria and lysosomes organelles interact with the endoplasmic reticulum (ER), often in tripartite contacts (Boutry and Kim, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR10 "Boutry M, Kim PK (2021) ORP1L mediated PI(4)P signaling at ER-lysosome-mitochondrion three-way contact contributes to mitochondrial division. Nat Commun 12:5354")) and also via signaling pathways (Agostini et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR2 "Agostini F, Pereyra L, Dale J, Yambire KF, Maglioni S, Schiavi A, Ventura N, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2024) Upregulation of cholesterol synthesis by lysosomal defects requires a functional mitochondrial respiratory chain. J Biol Chem 300:107403")). Mitochondrial and peroxisomal membrane dynamics, metabolism, and function are intertwined (DiGiovanni et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR36 "DiGiovanni LF, Khroud PK, Carmichael RE, Schrader TA, Gill SK, Germain K, Jomphe RY, Wiesinger C, Boutry M, Kamoshita M, Snider D, Stubbings G, Hua R, Garber N, Hacker C, Rutenberg AD, Melnyk RA, Berger J, Schrader M, Raught B, Kim PK (2025) ROS transfer at peroxisome-mitochondria contact regulates mitochondrial redox. Science 389:157–162"); Sugiura et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR123 "Sugiura A, Mattie S, Prudent J, McBride HM (2017) Newly born peroxisomes are a hybrid of mitochondrial and ER-derived pre-peroxisomes. Nature 542:251–254"); Coelho et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR25 "Coelho P, Dionísio P, A Sardão V, Dias RA, Futschik ME, Klemm R, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2026) Mitochondrial dysfunction triggers a maladaptive peroxisomal response driving lipid accumulation. Redox Biol 93:104166"); Nuebel et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR97 "Nuebel E, Morgan JT, Fogarty S, Winter JM, Lettlova S, Berg JA, Chen YC, Kidwell CU, Maschek JA, Clowers KJ, Argyriou C, Chen L, Wittig I, Cox JE, Roh-Johnson M, Braverman N, Bonkowsky J, Gygi SP, Rutter J (2021) The biochemical basis of mitochondrial dysfunction in Zellweger Spectrum Disorder. EMBO Rep 22:e51991")), and vesicles originating at the Golgi complex have a pivotal role in ER-mediated mitochondrial fission (Nagashima et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR87 "Nagashima S, Tábara LC, Tilokani L, Paupe V, Anand H, Pogson JH, Zunino R, McBride HM, Prudent J (2020) Golgi-derived PI(4)P-containing vesicles drive late steps of mitochondrial division. Science 367:1366–1371")).

Mitochondria and lysosomes have fundamental roles in cellular metabolism, and their dysfunctions result in severe metabolic diseases. While the prevailing epithet of mitochondria as ATP-producing factories remains popular, their roles vastly extend from that process, with their well-established involvement in apoptosis and cellular signaling, and impact on mitochondrial diseases as well as diabetes, cancer, and aging. Likewise, the perceived role of lysosomes has evolved from recycling center to a fundamental role in nutrient sensing, autophagy, and cellular logistics (e.g., ensuring that other organelles receive the building blocks they need), with dysfunctional lysosomes being an important driver of age-related neurodegenerative syndromes.

The importance of interactions between mitochondria and lysosomes is further evidenced by disease phenotypes, as primary defects in one of these organelles often result in secondary perturbations of the other (Deus et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR34 "Deus CM, Yambire KF, Oliveira PJ, Raimundo N (2020) Mitochondria-lysosome crosstalk: from physiology to neurodegeneration. Trends Mol Med 26:71–88")). Lysosomal storage diseases (e.g., Pompe’s disease) show abnormalities in peroxisomal metabolism, as well as in mitochondrial function, and mitochondrial biogenesis is systematically repressed in lysosomal lipid storage diseases. Reciprocally, mitochondrial myopathies (e.g., progressive external ophthalmoplegia) present impaired lysosomal autophagic capacity.

## Platforms of mitochondria–lysosome crosstalk

Mitochondria and lysosomes can communicate by multiple pathways (Fig.[1](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#Fig1)). Some are unidirectional, like mitophagy or mitochondria-derived vesicles (MDVs), as they carry cargo from mitochondria to lysosomes. Other pathways are bidirectional, allowing each organelle to communicate and impact the functionality of the other, such as signaling pathways and membrane contact sites.

**Figure 1: Pathways of communication between mitochondria and lysosomes.**

Mitochondria and lysosomes communicate through both degradative and non-degradative pathways that together coordinate cellular homeostasis. Degradative communication comprises mitophagy, which delivers whole mitochondria to lysosomes for degradation, and mitochondria-derived vesicles (MDVs), which selectively transport mitochondrial cargo for lysosomal degradation. Non-degradative communication includes membrane contact sites that enable the exchange of ions, lipids, and metabolites, as well as bidirectional signaling pathways that coordinate organelle adaptation through reactive oxygen species (ROS), amino acids, lysosomal Ca 2+, and signaling networks involving AMPK, mTORC1, and TFEB. Rather than functioning independently, these complementary communication modalities integrate mitochondrial and lysosomal activities to regulate metabolism, quality control, and stress adaptation. Disruption of one or more of these pathways contributes to the development of mitochondrial diseases, lysosomal disorders, neurodegeneration, cardiovascular disease, and aging.

### Mitophagy

Mitophagy is the selective autophagy of mitochondria (Lemasters, [2005](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR68 "Lemasters JJ (2005) Selective mitochondrial autophagy, or mitophagy, as a targeted defense against oxidative stress, mitochondrial dysfunction, and aging. Rejuvenation Res 8:3–5")) and occurs as a quality control mechanism to remove damaged mitochondria. This process involves the labeling of dysfunctional mitochondria, their packaging inside autophagosomes, fusion of the mitochondria-containing autophagosomes with lysosomes, and ultimately degradation of mitochondrial cargo by the lysosomes. There are multiple mechanisms for the labeling of mitochondria for mitophagy, which can be developmental (e.g., removal of mitochondria at the last step of erythrocyte development) or a part of stress responses (Onishi et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR99 "Onishi M, Yamano K, Sato M, Matsuda N, Okamoto K (2021) Molecular mechanisms and physiological functions of mitophagy. EMBO J 40:e104705")). The most widely studied mechanism of mitophagy is mediated by PINK1/Parkin. PINK1 is a kinase that, in healthy mitochondria, is imported in a potential-dependent manner to the inner mitochondrial membrane (IMM). In depolarized mitochondria, PINK1 cannot be imported to the IMM and instead accumulates on the outer mitochondrial membrane, where it recruits Parkin, an E3 ubiquitin ligase. Parkin polyubiquitinates many proteins at the outer mitochondrial membrane (OMM) (Narendra et al, [2008](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR89 "Narendra D, Tanaka A, Suen D-F, Youle RJ (2008) Parkin is recruited selectively to impaired mitochondria and promotes their autophagy. J Cell Biol 183:795–803"); Vives-Bauza et al, [2010](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR131 "Vives-Bauza C, Zhou C, Huang Y, Cui M, de Vries RLA, Kim J, May J, Tocilescu MA, Liu W, Ko HS et al (2010) PINK1-dependent recruitment of Parkin to mitochondria in mitophagy. Proc Natl Acad Sci USA 107:378–383"); Narendra et al, [2010](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR90 "Narendra DP, Jin SM, Tanaka A, Suen D-F, Gautier CA, Shen J, Cookson MR, Youle RJ (2010) PINK1 is selectively stabilized on impaired mitochondria to activate parkin. PLoS Biol 8:e1000298")), leading to the recruitment of autophagy adapters that promote autophagosome formation and ultimately degradation by the lysosome (Chan et al, [2011](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR19 "Chan NC, Salazar AM, Pham AH, Sweredoski MJ, Kolawa NJ, Graham RLJ, Hess S, Chan DC (2011) Broad activation of the ubiquitin–proteasome system by Parkin is critical for mitophagy. Hum Mol Genet 20:1726–1737"); Poole et al, [2010](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR108 "Poole AC, Thomas RE, Yu S, Vincow ES, Pallanck L (2010) The mitochondrial fusion-promoting factor mitofusin is a substrate of the PINK1/Parkin pathway. PLoS One 5:e10054")). Other mechanisms of mitophagy include BNIP3 and FUNDC1, which are most associated with stress conditions, as well as NIX, which is necessary for mitophagy in reticulocytes (Chen et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR21 "Chen M, Chen Z, Wang Y, Tan Z, Zhu C, Li Y, Han Z, Chen L, Gao R, Liu L et al (2016) Mitophagy receptor FUNDC1 regulates mitochondrial dynamics and mitophagy. Autophagy 12:689–702"); Schweers et al, [2007](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR117 "Schweers RL, Zhang J, Randall MS, Loyd MR, Li W, Dorsey FC, Kundu M, Opferman JT, Cleveland JL, Miller JL et al (2007) NIX is required for programmed mitochondrial clearance during reticulocyte maturation. Proc Natl Acad Sci USA 104:19500–19505"); Hanna et al, [2012](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR44 "Hanna RA, Quinsay MN, Orogo AM, Giang K, Rikka S, Gustafsson ÅB (2012) Microtubule-associated protein 1 light chain 3 (LC3) interacts with Bnip3 protein to selectively remove endoplasmic reticulum and mitochondria via autophagy. J Biol Chem 287:19094–19104"); Guo et al, [2001](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR43 "Guo K, Searfoss G, Krolikowski D, Pagnoni M, Franks C, Clark K, Yu KT, Jaye M, Ivashchenko Y (2001) Hypoxia induces the expression of the pro-apoptotic gene BNIP3. Cell Death Differ 8:367–376")). Fusion of mitophagosomes with lysosomes is thought to rely on the same core SNARE machinery used by bulk autophagosomes, involving STX17, SNAP29, and VAMP8 in a RAB7-dependent manner, rather than on a mitophagy-specific fusion apparatus. Whether mitophagosomes nonetheless require additional or distinct membrane labeling for efficient fusion, and whether they are preferentially delivered to a specialized lysosomal subpopulation competent to process mitochondrial cargo such as mtDNA, remains to be determined. Piecemeal mitophagy, in which only part of the mitochondrial organelle is degraded, has also been described and appears to depend specifically on the ATG8 family member LC3C rather than GABARAP subfamily proteins, indicating that distinct ATG8 paralogs can define mechanistically separate mitophagy sub-pathways (Le Guerroué et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR65 "Le Guerroué F, Eck F, Jung J, Starzetz T, Mittelbronn M, Kaulich M, Behrends C (2017) Autophagosomal content profiling reveals an LC3C-dependent piecemeal mitophagy pathway. Mol Cell 68:786–796.e6")).

Collectively, these observations highlight that mitophagy is not solely a degradative pathway, but also a mechanism for information transfer whereby mitochondrial macromolecules shape lysosomal signaling and inflammatory responses. Notably, this cargo is processed as information: mitochondrial DNA (mtDNA) is poorly methylated, similarly to bacterial DNA, and can be sensed by the lysosomal sensor of microbial DNA, TLR9 (toll-like receptor 9) (Latz et al, [2004](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR64 "Latz E, Schoenemeyer A, Visintin A, Fitzgerald KA, Monks BG, Knetter CF, Lien E, Nilsen NJ, Espevik T, Golenbock DT (2004) TLR9 signals after translocating from the ER to CpG DNA in the lysosome. Nat Immunol 5:190–198")); mitochondrial RNA is sensed as single-stranded RNA (ssRNA) by TLR7 and TLR8 (in humans, as TLR8 seems to be inactive in mice, (Liu et al, [2010](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR73 "Liu J, Xu C, Hsu L-C, Luo Y, Xiang R, Chuang T-H (2010) A five-amino-acid motif in the undefined region of the TLR8 ectodomain is required for species-specific ligand recognition. Mol Immunol 47:1083–1090"); Nunes et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR98 "Nunes IV, Breitenbach L, Pawusch S, Eigenbrod T, Ananth S, Schad P, Fackler OT, Butter F, Dalpke AH, Chen L-S (2024) Bacterial RNA sensing by TLR8 requires RNase 6 processing and is inhibited by RNA 2’O-methylation. EMBO Rep 25:4674–4692"))). The recognition of DNA or ssRNA by lysosomal TLRs results in their dimerization and consequent recruitment of the MYD88 adapter protein, leading to activation of the transcription factor NF-κB, which induces pro-inflammatory cytokine production (like TNF-α, IL-1, IL-6) (Sartorius et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR114 "Sartorius R, Trovato M, Manco R, D’Apice L, De Berardinis P (2021) Exploiting viral sensing mediated by Toll-like receptors to design innovative vaccines. NPJ Vaccines 6:127")). Notably, the delivery of mtDNA to lysosomes by basal autophagy is important for lysosomal homeostasis (De Leo et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR31 "De Leo MG, Staiano L, Vicinanza M, Luciani A, Carissimo A, Mutarelli M, Di Campli A, Polishchuk E, Di Tullio G, Morra V et al (2016) Autophagosome-lysosome fusion triggers a lysosomal response mediated by TLR9 and controlled by OCRL. Nat Cell Biol 18:839–850")), highlighting that the communication between these organelles takes place both in basal physiological conditions as well as in stress-induced states.

Furthermore, mitophagy can also follow the unconventional secretion pathway. When cells are unable to fuse autophagosomes with lysosomes, the secretion of mitochondrial proteins via VAMP7-dependent late endosomal trafficking is enhanced, providing an alternative route for mitochondrial content to reach the extracellular space (Vats et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR129 "Vats S, Dionisio P, Lemercier Q, Pineau R, Therreau L, Lipecka J, Cholley B, Moog J-B, Wojnacki J, Keime C et al (2026) VAMP7-dependent late endosomal secretion of ER and mitochondrial proteins impacts the tumor microenvironment and macrophage engagement. Nat Commun 17:3012")).

### MDVs

MDVs are small (~70–100 nm), cargo-selective carriers that bud from mitochondrial membranes independently of the core mitochondrial fission and autophagy machinery. Across the contexts studied so far, MDV biogenesis follows a common sequence—selection of specific mitochondrial cargo, tubulation of the donor membrane, scission of the vesicle, and directed trafficking to a target organelle—though the molecular determinants of each step differ depending on cargo and destination (König and McBride, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR61 "König T, McBride HM (2024) Mitochondrial-derived vesicles in metabolism, disease, and aging. Cell Metab 36:21–35")). They were first identified in 2008 (Neuspiel et al, [2008](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR92 "Neuspiel M, Schauss AC, Braschi E, Zunino R, Rippstein P, Rachubinski RA, Andrade-Navarro MA, McBride HM (2008) Cargo-selected transport from the mitochondria to peroxisomes is mediated by vesicular carriers. Curr Biol 18:102–108")). MDVs can be delivered to various intracellular destinations, and more research is being performed looking at associations between certain cargo and trafficking routes. For example, peroxisomal delivery has been linked to recruitment of the mitochondrial-anchored protein ligase (MAPL) (Braschi et al, [2010](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR11 "Braschi E, Goyon V, Zunino R, Mohanty A, Xu L, McBride HM (2010) Vps35 mediates vesicle transport between the mitochondria and peroxisomes. Curr Biol 20:1310–1315")). In addition, research has linked MDVs that are MAPL-negative but TOMM20-positive (i.e., contain mitochondrial outer membrane) as destined for the lysosome (Neuspiel et al, [2008](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR92 "Neuspiel M, Schauss AC, Braschi E, Zunino R, Rippstein P, Rachubinski RA, Andrade-Navarro MA, McBride HM (2008) Cargo-selected transport from the mitochondria to peroxisomes is mediated by vesicular carriers. Curr Biol 18:102–108")). MAPL and TOMM20 status are best regarded as markers of cargo composition associated with these two routes rather than fixed determinants of trafficking fate, and it remains to be established how generalizable this dichotomy is across cell types and stimuli. While MDV formation was initially thought to be independent of the mitochondrial fission mediator DRP1 (dynamin-related protein 1), more recent work shows that DRP1 and associated adapters (MID49/MID51/MFF) can drive the biogenesis of certain TOMM20-positive MDVs from mitochondrial membranes, with MIRO1/2 and microtubule-based transport guiding protrusion and scission events (König et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR62 "König T, Nolte H, Aaltonen MJ, Tatsuta T, Krols M, Stroh T, Langer T, McBride HM (2021) MIROs and DRP1 drive mitochondrial-derived vesicle biogenesis and promote quality control. Nat Cell Biol 23:1271–1286")). This MIRO-dependent tubulation mechanism appears to be evolutionarily ancient: vesicle-like structures budding from mitochondrial outer-membrane protrusions were first described in Arabidopsis (Yamashita et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR140 "Yamashita A, Fujimoto M, Katayama K, Yamaoka S, Tsutsumi N, Arimura S (2016) Formation of mitochondrial outer membrane derived protrusions and vesicles in Arabidopsis thaliana. PLoS One 11:e0146717")), well before the mechanistic dissection of an analogous MIRO1/2-dependent pathway in animal cells.

Lysosomal delivery of MDVs does not require depolarization of the mitochondria or autophagic proteins that are typically required for mitophagy (Soubannier et al, [2012a](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR121 "Soubannier V, McLelland G-L, Zunino R, Braschi E, Rippstein P, Fon EA, McBride HM (2012a) A vesicular transport pathway shuttles cargo from mitochondria to lysosomes. Curr Biol 22:135–141")). Oxidative stress can stimulate the formation of MDVs enriched in oxidized proteins (Soubannier et al, [2012b](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR122 "Soubannier V, Rippstein P, Kaufman BA, Shoubridge EA, McBride HM (2012b) Reconstitution of mitochondria derived vesicle formation demonstrates selective enrichment of oxidized cargo. PLoS One 7:e52830")), a process dependent on PINK1-dependent Parkin recruitment to MDVs, which are also labeled with the SNARE protein STX17 (McLelland et al, [2014](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR83 "McLelland G-L, Soubannier V, Chen CX, McBride HM, Fon EA (2014) Parkin and PINK1 function in a vesicular trafficking pathway regulating mitochondrial quality control. EMBO J 33:282–295")). Several factors have been implicated in both MDV generation and trafficking, including RAB9 and SNX9 in vesicle formation and STX17 and the HOPS complex in vesicle delivery (McLelland et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR82 "McLelland G-L, Lee SA, McBride HM, Fon EA (2016) Syntaxin-17 delivers PINK1/parkin-dependent mitochondrial vesicles to the endolysosomal system. J Cell Biol 214:275–291")). Whether these components define a distinct, MIRO/DRP1-independent biogenesis route, or instead act downstream in the trafficking of MIRO-generated MDVs toward the lysosome, remains unresolved. It is not yet clear whether MDV biogenesis proceeds through a single core mechanism with cargo- or stimulus-specific accessory factors (e.g., MIRO1/2 for lysosome-destined MDVs versus VPS35 for peroxisome-destined MDVs), or through mechanistically distinct pathways that converge only at the point of cargo delivery (Matheoud et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR80 "Matheoud D, Sugiura A, Bellemare-Pelletier A, Laplante A, Rondeau C, Chemali M, Fazel A, Bergeron JJ, Trudeau L-E, Burelle Y et al (2016) Parkinson’s disease-related proteins PINK1 and Parkin repress mitochondrial antigen presentation. Cell 166:314–327"); McLelland et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR82 "McLelland G-L, Lee SA, McBride HM, Fon EA (2016) Syntaxin-17 delivers PINK1/parkin-dependent mitochondrial vesicles to the endolysosomal system. J Cell Biol 214:275–291")). MDVs can carry a wide array of cargo, including mtDNA, oxidized proteins, redox enzymes, and metabolic enzymes (Ng and Tang, [2014](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR94 "Ng F, Tang BL (2014) Pyruvate dehydrogenase complex (PDC) export from the mitochondrial matrix. Mol Membr Biol 31:207–210"); Picca et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR105 "Picca A, Beli R, Calvani R, Coelho-Júnior HJ, Landi F, Bernabei R, Bucci C, Guerra F, Marzetti E (2020) Older adults with physical frailty and sarcopenia show increased levels of circulating small extracellular vesicles with a specific mitochondrial signature. Cells 9:973"); Soubannier et al, [2012b](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR122 "Soubannier V, Rippstein P, Kaufman BA, Shoubridge EA, McBride HM (2012b) Reconstitution of mitochondria derived vesicle formation demonstrates selective enrichment of oxidized cargo. PLoS One 7:e52830"); Vasam et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR128 "Vasam G, Nadeau R, Cadete VJJ, Lavallée-Adam M, Menzies KJ, Burelle Y (2021) Proteomics characterization of mitochondrial-derived vesicles under oxidative stress. FASEB J 35:e21278")).

MDVs have various biological roles within the cell. One of their major roles is as a mitochondrial quality control mechanism to remove segments of damaged mitochondria without degrading the whole organelle. In cellular stress conditions, MDV formation is shown to increase, and MDV production can compensate for mitophagy loss in cells (Towers et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR127 "Towers CG, Wodetzki DK, Thorburn J, Smith KR, Caino MC, Thorburn A (2021) Mitochondrial derived vesicles compensate for loss of LC3-mediated mitophagy. Dev Cell 56:2029–2042.e5"); Cadete et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR14 "Cadete VJJ, Deschênes S, Cuillerier A, Brisebois F, Sugiura A, Vincent A, Turnbull D, Picard M, McBride HM, Burelle Y (2016) Formation of mitochondrial-derived vesicles is an active and physiologically relevant mitochondrial quality control process in the cardiac system. J Physiol 594:5343–5362")). This highlights the role played by MDVs in promoting mitochondrial health, complementary to mitophagy. MDVs have also been described to play a part in mitochondrial antigen presentation (MitAP), a process essential for immune responses. In the absence of PINK1 and Parkin, MitAP was promoted by the formation of MDVs, which required SNX9 and RAB9 (Matheoud et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR80 "Matheoud D, Sugiura A, Bellemare-Pelletier A, Laplante A, Rondeau C, Chemali M, Fazel A, Bergeron JJ, Trudeau L-E, Burelle Y et al (2016) Parkinson’s disease-related proteins PINK1 and Parkin repress mitochondrial antigen presentation. Cell 166:314–327")). A recent study has also found a role for MDVs in the delivery of MFF to damaged lysosomes in response to hypoxia-reoxygenation stress to promote budding-type fission (Luo et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR75 "Luo Y, Yu J, Li Z, Li W, Jiang L, Huang C, Rong Z, Lin L, Rong Y, Yan C, Chen Z, Tang J, He H, Shi A, Song Z (2026) Mitochondrial-derived vesicles drive budding-type fission of damaged lysosomes. Nat Cell Biol 28:1686–1699")).

The mitochondrial cargoes that are delivered by MDVs to the lysosome undergo similar processing as mitophagy cargo, but it remains unclear how each of these pathways contributes to lysosomal signaling and mitochondria–lysosome-mediated inflammation. It also remains unexplored if the lipids that are carried from mitochondria to lysosomes are sensed by the lysosomes or simply processed by the lysosomal lipases (Domingues et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR37 "Domingues N, Pires J, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2025) Role of lipids in interorganelle communication. Trends Cell Biol 35:46–58")). In particular, while the contents of the MDVs are subject to lysosomal degradation, the MDV membrane has to fuse with the lysosomal membrane, possibly altering the latter in small microdomains, a question that remains unaddressed. Along these lines, the machinery mediating the fusion between MDVs and the lysosomal membrane remains poorly understood. While the SNARE protein STX17 has been associated with one particular type of MDVs (McLelland et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR82 "McLelland G-L, Lee SA, McBride HM, Fon EA (2016) Syntaxin-17 delivers PINK1/parkin-dependent mitochondrial vesicles to the endolysosomal system. J Cell Biol 214:275–291")), suggesting a pathway of fusion with the lysosomal membrane akin to that used by autophagosomes, it remains unclear how MDVs lacking STX17 fuse with the lysosome or if alternative delivery pathways exist. An example of this uncharted territory is the description of a “mitochondria–lysosome-related organelle” (MLRO), an electron-dense organelle that can have either a single or double membrane containing both mitochondria and lysosome markers (Ma et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR77 "Ma X, Manley S, Qian H, Li Y, Zhang C, Li K, Ding B, Guo F, Chen A, Zhang X et al (2023) Mitochondria-lysosome-related organelles mediate mitochondrial clearance during cellular dedifferentiation. Cell Rep 42:113291")). These “MLROs” may result from the fusion of MDVs with lysosomes through a PARKIN-, ATG5-, and DRP1-independent process (Ma et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR77 "Ma X, Manley S, Qian H, Li Y, Zhang C, Li K, Ding B, Guo F, Chen A, Zhang X et al (2023) Mitochondria-lysosome-related organelles mediate mitochondrial clearance during cellular dedifferentiation. Cell Rep 42:113291")). These organelle hybrids were described in dedifferentiating hepatocytes, and their formation is negatively regulated by TFEB, linking them to lysosomal homeostasis (Ma et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR77 "Ma X, Manley S, Qian H, Li Y, Zhang C, Li K, Ding B, Guo F, Chen A, Zhang X et al (2023) Mitochondria-lysosome-related organelles mediate mitochondrial clearance during cellular dedifferentiation. Cell Rep 42:113291")). Further studies will clarify their nature, origin, and occurrence in other cell types.

The portioning of selected mitochondrial cargo into MDVs towards the lysosome is important to avoid its delivery to multivesicular bodies (MVB) and their eventual exocytosis (D’Acunzo et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR28 "D’Acunzo P, Pérez-González R, Kim Y, Hargash T, Miller C, Alldred MJ, Erdjument-Bromage H, Penikalapati SC, Pawlik M, Saito M et al (2021) Mitovesicles are a novel population of extracellular vesicles of mitochondrial origin altered in Down syndrome. Sci Adv 7:eabe5085")). The secretion of mitochondrial cargo packaged in extracellular vesicles (EVs) (Wojnacki et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR134 "Wojnacki J, Nola S, Bun P, Cholley B, Filippini F, Pressé MT, Lipecka J, Man Lam S, N’guyen J, Simon A et al (2020) Role of VAMP7-dependent secretion of reticulon 3 in neurite growth. Cell Rep 33:108536"); Vats et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR129 "Vats S, Dionisio P, Lemercier Q, Pineau R, Therreau L, Lipecka J, Cholley B, Moog J-B, Wojnacki J, Keime C et al (2026) VAMP7-dependent late endosomal secretion of ER and mitochondrial proteins impacts the tumor microenvironment and macrophage engagement. Nat Commun 17:3012")) or as soluble proteins serves as a potential step to regulate the uncontrolled release of potentially inflammagenic contents to the extracellular environment (Todkar et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR126 "Todkar K, Chikhi L, Desjardins V, El-Mortada F, Pépin G, Germain M (2021) Selective packaging of mitochondrial proteins into extracellular vesicles prevents the release of mitochondrial DAMPs. Nat Commun 12:1971")).

A unifying theme emerges from the yet sparse literature on MDVs: rather than passively releasing mitochondrial contents, cells actively sort specific cargo into distinct vesicle populations depending on physiological context. MDVs can direct damaged or oxidized components toward lysosomal degradation to prevent inflammatory signaling, while selectively packaging specific mitochondrial proteins into extracellular vesicles for controlled release. Similarly, SNARE-dependent secretion pathways such as those mediated by VAMP7 regulate the targeted export of specific proteins to shape cellular behavior and intercellular communication. Together, these studies establish MDVs as selective communication vesicles rather than merely intermediates of mitochondrial quality control. By directing specific mitochondrial cargoes toward lysosomal degradation or extracellular release, MDVs enable cells to actively determine the fate and signaling potential of mitochondrial components according to physiological context. MDVs have also recently been shown to actively remodel their target organelle rather than simply deliver cargo to it: MDVs carrying the fission adapter MFF are targeted to damaged lysosomes via a MIRO2–ITM2C tethering interaction, where MFF recruits DRP1 to drive a budding-type lysosomal fission that restores lysosomal function after hypoxia–reoxygenation stress (Luo et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR75 "Luo Y, Yu J, Li Z, Li W, Jiang L, Huang C, Rong Z, Lin L, Rong Y, Yan C, Chen Z, Tang J, He H, Shi A, Song Z (2026) Mitochondrial-derived vesicles drive budding-type fission of damaged lysosomes. Nat Cell Biol 28:1686–1699")). This finding extends MDV-mediated mitochondria–lysosome communication beyond degradative cargo transfer to direct control of lysosomal quality control. Available evidence indicates that MDV production is primarily an acute, stress-responsive program: MDV output rises within minutes of acute bioenergetic stress, preceding the activation of mitophagy at later time points (McLelland et al, [2014](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR83 "McLelland G-L, Soubannier V, Chen CX, McBride HM, Fon EA (2014) Parkin and PINK1 function in a vesicular trafficking pathway regulating mitochondrial quality control. EMBO J 33:282–295")). The open question is therefore not whether MDVs constitute an acute response, but whether and how this program is remodeled or becomes dysregulated during chronic disease, and how such remodeling contributes to pathology.

### Signaling

Beyond direct cargo transfer, mitochondria and lysosomes communicate through an intricate signaling network that integrates metabolic state, nutrient availability, and cellular stress to coordinate organelle adaptation. Acute mitochondrial respiratory chain inhibition triggers lysosomal biogenesis (Fernández-Mosquera et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR39 "Fernández-Mosquera L, Diogo CV, Yambire KF, Santos GL, Luna Sánchez M, Bénit P, Rustin P, Lopez LC, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2017) Acute and chronic mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency differentially regulate lysosomal biogenesis. Sci Rep 7:45076")), which is possibly related to the reported stimulation of lysosomal Ca 2+ release through the transporter TRPML1 (mucolipin 1, also known as MCOLN1) by reactive oxygen species (ROS) (Zhang et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR147 "Zhang X, Cheng X, Yu L, Yang J, Calvo R, Patnaik S, Hu X, Gao Q, Yang M, Lawas M et al (2016) MCOLN1 is a ROS sensor in lysosomes that regulates autophagy. Nat Commun 7:12109")). However, chronic mitochondrial perturbations, either stemming from loss of mtDNA (Baixauli et al, [2015](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR6 "Baixauli F, Acín-Pérez R, Villarroya-Beltrí C, Mazzeo C, Nuñez-Andrade N, Gabandé-Rodriguez E, Ledesma MD, Blázquez A, Martin MA, Falcón-Pérez JM et al (2015) Mitochondrial respiration controls lysosomal function during inflammatory T cell responses. Cell Metab 22:485–498")), respiratory chain malfunction (Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591")), or impaired mitochondrial dynamics (Demers-Lamarche et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR32 "Demers-Lamarche J, Guillebaud G, Tlili M, Todkar K, Bélanger N, Grondin M, Nguyen AP, Michel J, Germain M (2016) Loss of mitochondrial function impairs lysosomes. J Biol Chem 291:10263–10276")), result in lysosomal dysfunction and impaired lysosomal biogenesis. The perturbation of lysosomes under chronic mitochondrial defects is associated with lower levels of lysosomal phospholipid PtdIns(3,5)P 2, increased lysosomal Ca 2+ storage, lower lysosomal hydrolytic activity (Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591")), and perturbation of lysosomal lipid handling (Agostini et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR2 "Agostini F, Pereyra L, Dale J, Yambire KF, Maglioni S, Schiavi A, Ventura N, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2024) Upregulation of cholesterol synthesis by lysosomal defects requires a functional mitochondrial respiratory chain. J Biol Chem 300:107403"); Baixauli et al, [2015](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR6 "Baixauli F, Acín-Pérez R, Villarroya-Beltrí C, Mazzeo C, Nuñez-Andrade N, Gabandé-Rodriguez E, Ledesma MD, Blázquez A, Martin MA, Falcón-Pérez JM et al (2015) Mitochondrial respiration controls lysosomal function during inflammatory T cell responses. Cell Metab 22:485–498")). Notably, stimulation of lysosomal Ca 2+ efflux through the lysosomal TRPML1 channel is sufficient to correct key phenotypes in cells and animals with chronic mitochondrial defects (Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591"); Agostini et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR2 "Agostini F, Pereyra L, Dale J, Yambire KF, Maglioni S, Schiavi A, Ventura N, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2024) Upregulation of cholesterol synthesis by lysosomal defects requires a functional mitochondrial respiratory chain. J Biol Chem 300:107403")).

The signaling pathway AMPK and the transcription factor TFEB also play important roles in the communication between mitochondria and lysosomes. Acute mitochondrial stress promotes AMPK signaling and activates TFEB nuclear localization and transcriptional activity (Fernández-Mosquera et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR39 "Fernández-Mosquera L, Diogo CV, Yambire KF, Santos GL, Luna Sánchez M, Bénit P, Rustin P, Lopez LC, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2017) Acute and chronic mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency differentially regulate lysosomal biogenesis. Sci Rep 7:45076"); Nezich et al, [2015](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR93 "Nezich CL, Wang C, Fogel AI, Youle RJ (2015) MiT/TFE transcription factors are activated during mitophagy downstream of Parkin and Atg5. J Cell Biol 210:435–450")), while both AMPK and TFEB activities are downregulated in chronic mitochondrial perturbations (Viscomi et al, [2011](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR130 "Viscomi C, Bottani E, Civiletto G, Cerutti R, Moggio M, Fagiolari G, Schon EA, Lamperti C, Zeviani M (2011) In vivo correction of COX deficiency by activation of the AMPK/PGC-1α axis. Cell Metab 14:80–90"); Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591")). AMPK stimulates both mitochondrial biogenesis (Malik et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR79 "Malik N, Ferreira BI, Hollstein PE, Curtis SD, Trefts E, Weiser Novak S, Yu J, Gilson R, Hellberg K, Fang L et al (2023) Induction of lysosomal and mitochondrial biogenesis by AMPK phosphorylation of FNIP1. Science 380:eabj5559"); Bergeron et al, [2001](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR8 "Bergeron R, Ren JM, Cadman KS, Moore IK, Perret P, Pypaert M, Young LH, Semenkovich CF, Shulman GI (2001) Chronic activation of AMP kinase results in NRF-1 activation and mitochondrial biogenesis. Am J Physiol Endocrinol Metab 281:E1340–E1346")) and TFEB-dependent lysosomal biogenesis (Young et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR144 "Young NP, Kamireddy A, Van Nostrand JL, Eichner LJ, Shokhirev MN, Dayn Y, Shaw RJ (2016) AMPK governs lineage specification through Tfeb-dependent regulation of lysosomes. Genes Dev 30:535–552")) and also promotes lysosomal function (Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591")). Interestingly, AMPK-stimulated PtdIns(3,5)P 2 production at the lysosomes promotes efflux of Ca 2+ through the lysosomal TRPML1 (Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591")), which leads to activation of calcineurin and dephosphorylation of TFEB (Medina et al, [2015](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR84 "Medina DL, Di Paola S, Peluso I, Armani A, De Stefani D, Venditti R, Montefusco S, Scotto-Rosato A, Prezioso C, Forrester A et al (2015) Lysosomal calcium signalling regulates autophagy through calcineurin and TFEB. Nat Cell Biol 17:288–299")). Both TFEB and its closely related family member TFE3 activate transcriptional programs of mitochondrial and lysosomal biogenesis (Pastore et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR100 "Pastore N, Vainshtein A, Klisch TJ, Armani A, Huynh T, Herz NJ, Polishchuk EV, Sandri M, Ballabio A (2017) TFE3 regulates whole-body energy metabolism in cooperation with TFEB. EMBO Mol Med 9:605–621")). Notably, acute inhibition or depolarization of mitochondria, leading to increased generation of reactive oxygen species (ROS), has been shown to increase the activity of the TRPML1 channel, leading to the hypothesis that TRPML1 might act as a sensor for mitochondrial ROS (Zhang et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR147 "Zhang X, Cheng X, Yu L, Yang J, Calvo R, Patnaik S, Hu X, Gao Q, Yang M, Lawas M et al (2016) MCOLN1 is a ROS sensor in lysosomes that regulates autophagy. Nat Commun 7:12109")). It remains, however, to be determined which ROS species would specifically stimulate TRPML1 and whether this would be a direct or indirect interaction. Furthermore, the involvement of TRPML1 activity in the membrane contact sites between mitochondria and lysosomes, to be discussed in more detail below, raises the importance of TRPML1 and lysosomal Ca 2+ release in the communication between mitochondria and lysosomes.

A recurring principle emerging from these studies is that the outcome of mitochondria–lysosome communication is highly dependent on the duration of the stimulus. Acute mitochondrial stress activates adaptive lysosomal programs that restore cellular homeostasis, whereas persistent mitochondrial dysfunction progressively dismantles these same communication pathways, establishing self-reinforcing cycles of organelle dysfunction.

Lysosomal behavior can also affect mitochondrial function, both via decreased mitochondrial quality control (impaired autophagy and consequently impaired mitophagy) and mitophagy-independent pathways. Lysosomal lipid storage disorders trigger a repression of the transcriptional programs of mitochondrial biogenesis (Yambire et al, [2019a](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR141 "Yambire KF, Fernandez-Mosquera L, Steinfeld R, Mühle C, Ikonen E, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019a) Mitochondrial biogenesis is transcriptionally repressed in lysosomal lipid storage diseases. eLife 8:e39598")), possibly as an attempt to compensate for decreased autophagy/mitophagy capacity. Loss of function of GBA1 (glucocerebrosidase), the lysosomal enzyme that hydrolyzes glucosylceramide into ceramide and glucose, has well-established consequences for mitochondrial structure, function, and quality control (Gegg and Schapira, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR41 "Gegg ME, Schapira AHV (2016) Mitochondrial dysfunction associated with glucocerebrosidase deficiency. Neurobiol Dis 90:43–50")). The role of GBA1-induced perturbations in mitochondrial-lysosome crosstalk and their involvement in the pathological mechanisms underlying Parkinson’s disease will be discussed further below.

The perturbation of lysosomal acidification also impacts mitochondria. Chronic inhibition of the v-ATPase has been shown to lead to mitochondrial fragmentation and loss of mitochondrial respiratory capacity with increased superoxide generation, due to functional iron-deficiency (Yambire et al, [2019b](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR142 "Yambire KF, Rostosky C, Watanabe T, Pacheu-Grau D, Torres-Odio S, Sanchez-Guerrero A, Senderovich O, Meyron-Holtz EG, Milosevic I, Frahm J et al (2019b) Impaired lysosomal acidification triggers iron deficiency and inflammation in vivo. eLife 8:e51031")) (Weber et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR133 "Weber RA, Yen FS, Nicholson SPV, Alwaseem H, Bayraktar EC, Alam M, Timson RC, La K, Abu-Remaileh M, Molina H et al (2020) Maintaining iron homeostasis is the key role of lysosomal acidity for cell proliferation. Mol Cell 77:645–655.e7")). Lysosomal acidification is also involved in the transcriptional activation of the mitochondrial unfolded protein response (mitoUPR), a key mechanism to respond to mitochondrial stress (Li et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR70 "Li TY, Wang Q, Gao AW, Li X, Sun Y, Mottis A, Shong M, Auwerx J (2023) Lysosomes mediate the mitochondrial UPR via mTORC1-dependent ATF4 phosphorylation. Cell Discov 9:92")). Interestingly, inhibition of the v-ATPase, via pharmacological or genetic approaches, results in increased potential across the mitochondrial inner membrane (Monteuuis et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR86 "Monteuuis G, Awadhpersad R, van der Kolk D, Singh SK, Nyman TA, Malyutina A, Zamboni N, Moisio K, Juutila J, Hietakangas V et al (2025) Vacuolar-type H+-ATPase-mediated extra-organellar buffering resolves mitochondrial dysfunction. Nat Commun 17:67")). This effect has been harnessed to increase mitochondrial potential via inhibition of the v-ATPase in cellular models of mitochondrial disease (Monteuuis et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR86 "Monteuuis G, Awadhpersad R, van der Kolk D, Singh SK, Nyman TA, Malyutina A, Zamboni N, Moisio K, Juutila J, Hietakangas V et al (2025) Vacuolar-type H+-ATPase-mediated extra-organellar buffering resolves mitochondrial dysfunction. Nat Commun 17:67")). While the mechanisms underlying this mitochondrial hyperpolarization and its biological implications remain unclear, this finding further reinforces the intertwined relationship between mitochondria and lysosomes.

Beyond alterations in lysosomal acidification or nutrient signaling, physical damage to the lysosomal membrane itself constitutes a distinct route by which lysosomes signal to mitochondria. Limited, sub-lethal lysosomal membrane permeabilization—triggered by intracellular pathogens, crystalline particulates or lysosomotropic agents—allows leakage of lysosomal cathepsins into the cytosol, which proteolytically remodel the mitochondrial proteome, including turnover of TOM20, MFN2 and TIM23, independently of mitophagy, mitochondrial proteases or the proteasome, thereby reprogramming macrophage immunometabolism and host responses to infection (Bussi et al, [2022](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR12 "Bussi C, Heunis T, Pellegrino E, Bernard EM, Bah N, Silva Dos Santos M, Santucci P, Aylan B, Rodgers A, Fearns A, Mitschke J, Moore C, MacRae JI, Greco M, Reinheckel T, Trost M, Gutierrez MG (2022) Lysosomal damage drives mitochondrial proteome remodelling and reprograms macrophage immunometabolism. Nat Commun 13:7338")). This lysosome-to-mitochondria damage signal can also be actively amplified by the immune system: following priming with interferon-γ, the antibacterial protein APOL3 senses lysosomal membrane damage and is recruited to mitochondria already undergoing BAK1/BAX-dependent outer membrane permeabilization, where it selectively permeabilizes the IMM by solubilizing cardiolipin, thereby potentiating mtDNA efflux and downstream cGAS–STING-dependent type I interferon production (Ritacco et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR110 "Ritacco DA, Shahnawaz H, Oduguwa A, Hawk J, Vizcaino B, Farber DL, Gaudet RG (2026) The human antibacterial factor APOL3 couples lysosomal damage to mitochondrial DNA efflux and type I IFN induction. Mol Cell 86:1116–1133.e8")). Together, these findings identify lysosomal membrane integrity, rather than acidification or degradative capacity alone, as a further platform through which lysosomes shape mitochondrial function and couple organelle damage to innate immune signaling.

The lysosomal regulation of the nutrient sensor mTORC1, whose activation depends on lysosomal function, has also been shown to mediate mitochondrial behaviors. Activation of mTORC1 at the lysosomes by amino acids stimulates mitochondrial activity (Norambuena et al, [2018](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR96 "Norambuena A, Wallrabe H, Cao R, Wang DB, Silva A, Svindrych Z, Periasamy A, Hu S, Tanzi RE, Kim DY et al (2018) A novel lysosome-to-mitochondria signaling pathway disrupted by amyloid-β oligomers. EMBO J 37:e100241")), a mechanism that is perturbed in Alzheimer’s disease, and promotes mitoUPR (Li et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR70 "Li TY, Wang Q, Gao AW, Li X, Sun Y, Mottis A, Shong M, Auwerx J (2023) Lysosomes mediate the mitochondrial UPR via mTORC1-dependent ATF4 phosphorylation. Cell Discov 9:92")). However, acute mTORC1 inhibition improved cell survival in a model of mitochondrial disease caused by frataxin deficiency (Lehmer and Zoncu, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR67 "Lehmer M, Zoncu R (2024) mTORC1 signaling inhibition modulates mitochondrial function in frataxin deficiency. Preprint at bioRxiv 
                https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.08.06.606942
                
              ")) and alleviated the disease burden in a mouse model of mitochondrial encephalopathy (Johnson et al, [2013](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR54 "Johnson SC, Yanos ME, Kayser E-B, Quintana A, Sangesland M, Castanza A, Uhde L, Hui J, Wall VZ, Gagnidze A et al (2013) mTOR inhibition alleviates mitochondrial disease in a mouse model of Leigh syndrome. Science 342:1524–1528")). Although inhibition of mTORC1 with rapamycin has been shown to be beneficial in several mitochondrial encephalopathies and myopathies (Khan et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR57 "Khan NA, Nikkanen J, Yatsuga S, Jackson C, Wang L, Pradhan S, Kivelä R, Pessia A, Velagapudi V, Suomalainen A (2017) mTORC1 regulates mitochondrial integrated stress response and mitochondrial myopathy progression. Cell Metab 26:419–428.e5"); Johnson et al, [2013](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR54 "Johnson SC, Yanos ME, Kayser E-B, Quintana A, Sangesland M, Castanza A, Uhde L, Hui J, Wall VZ, Gagnidze A et al (2013) mTOR inhibition alleviates mitochondrial disease in a mouse model of Leigh syndrome. Science 342:1524–1528")), it is not a universal treatment for all mitochondrial diseases (Barriocanal-Casado et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR7 "Barriocanal-Casado E, Hidalgo-Gutiérrez A, Raimundo N, González-García P, Acuña-Castroviejo D, Escames G, López LC (2019) Rapamycin administration is not a valid therapeutic strategy for every case of mitochondrial disease. EBioMedicine 42:511–523"); Ignatenko et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR51 "Ignatenko O, Nikkanen J, Kononov A, Zamboni N, Ince-Dunn G, Suomalainen A (2020) Mitochondrial spongiotic brain disease: astrocytic stress and harmful rapamycin and ketosis effect. Life Sci Alliance 3:e202000797")). Overall, these studies imply the existence of an optimal range of mTORC1 signaling and how mitochondria can be perturbed by either a lack or an excess of lysosomal mTORC1 signaling. Interestingly, the benefits of inhibiting mTORC1 in the context of mitochondrial disease raise the question of its antagonistic relationship with AMPK. Inhibition of mTORC1 is expected to result in activation of AMPK, and it is therefore noteworthy that stimulation of AMPK activity was shown to be beneficial in both cells, worms, and myopathy mouse models with mitochondrial respiratory chain defects (Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591"); Viscomi et al, [2011](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR130 "Viscomi C, Bottani E, Civiletto G, Cerutti R, Moggio M, Fagiolari G, Schon EA, Lamperti C, Zeviani M (2011) In vivo correction of COX deficiency by activation of the AMPK/PGC-1α axis. Cell Metab 14:80–90")). The tissue-specificity of the presentations associated with the mitochondrial defects is likely to play an important role in determining how the balance of mTORC1-AMPK contributes to the underlying pathology: while AMPK activation seems beneficial in skeletal muscle (Viscomi et al, [2011](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR130 "Viscomi C, Bottani E, Civiletto G, Cerutti R, Moggio M, Fagiolari G, Schon EA, Lamperti C, Zeviani M (2011) In vivo correction of COX deficiency by activation of the AMPK/PGC-1α axis. Cell Metab 14:80–90")), it is often detrimental in neuronal cells (McKay et al, [2015](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR81 "McKay SE, Yan W, Nouws J, Thormann MJ, Raimundo N, Khan A, Santos-Sacchi J, Song L, Shadel GS (2015) Auditory pathology in a transgenic mtTFB1 mouse model of mitochondrial deafness. Am J Pathol 185:3132–3140"); Raimundo et al, [2012](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR109 "Raimundo N, Song L, Shutt TE, McKay SE, Cotney J, Guan M-X, Gilliland TC, Hohuan D, Santos-Sacchi J, Shadel GS (2012) Mitochondrial stress engages E2F1 apoptotic signaling to cause deafness. Cell 148:716–726"); Zhao et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR148 "Zhao J, Li G, Zhao X, Lin X, Gao Y, Raimundo N, Li G-L, Shang W, Wu H, Song L (2020) Down-regulation of AMPK signaling pathway rescues hearing loss in TFB1 transgenic mice and delays age-related hearing loss. Aging 12:5590–5611")).

### Membrane contact sites

Mitochondrial and lysosomal dysfunction have been observed together in multiple disease states, suggesting coordination between the two organelles to maintain proper cellular functions. The unveiling of membrane contact sites linking the two organelles (M-L contacts) provides an additional platform of communication (Fig.[2](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#Fig2)). M-L contacts are RAB7-GTP-dependent (Wong et al, [2018](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR138 "Wong YC, Ysselstein D, Krainc D (2018) Mitochondria-lysosome contacts regulate mitochondrial fission via RAB7 GTP hydrolysis. Nature 554:382–386")). Lysosomal GTP-bound RAB7 binds to a still undetermined mitochondrial partner protein to promote tethering, while untethering is promoted by Fis1 on the outer mitochondrial membrane, which recruits TBC1D15, a RAB7 GAP, promoting the conversion of RAB7-GTP into RAB7-GDP and consequent M-L contact untethering (Wong et al, [2018](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR138 "Wong YC, Ysselstein D, Krainc D (2018) Mitochondria-lysosome contacts regulate mitochondrial fission via RAB7 GTP hydrolysis. Nature 554:382–386")). It is important to note that M-L contacts are distinct from both MDVs and mitophagy, as there are no autophagosome markers present and no bulk transfer seen of mitochondrial or lysosomal content (Wong et al, [2018](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR138 "Wong YC, Ysselstein D, Krainc D (2018) Mitochondria-lysosome contacts regulate mitochondrial fission via RAB7 GTP hydrolysis. Nature 554:382–386")).

**Figure 2: Molecular mechanisms at mitochondria–lysosome (M-L) membrane contact sites.**

M-L contact sites are dynamic hubs where ions, metabolites, and lipids pass between the two organelles. Calcium: Ca²⁺ released from the lysosome through the channel TRPML1 is taken up by mitochondria at contact sites, crossing the outer mitochondrial membrane through VDAC1 and the inner membrane through MCU (Peng et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR103 "Peng W, Wong YC, Krainc D (2020) Mitochondria-lysosome contacts regulate mitochondrial Ca2+ dynamics via lysosomal TRPML1. Proc Natl Acad Sci USA 117:19266–19275")). Iron: In melanoma cells, BDH2 localizes to M-L contacts and makes 2,5-dihydroxybenzoic acid (2,5-DHBA), an iron-binding molecule (siderophore) that carries lysosomal Fe²⁺ into mitochondria (Rizzollo et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR111 "Rizzollo F, Escamilla-Ayala A, Fattorelli N, Lysiak NB, More S, Hernández Varas P, Barazzuol L, Van den Haute C, Van Asselberghs J, Nittner D et al (2025) BDH2-driven lysosome-to-mitochondria iron transfer shapes ferroptosis vulnerability of the melanoma cell states. Nat Metab 7:1851–1870")). Amino acids: M-L contacts have been implicated in moving amino acids from lysosomes to mitochondria, a process promoted by Parkin’s stabilization of active, GTP-bound RAB7 (Peng et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR102 "Peng W, Schröder LF, Song P, Wong YC, Krainc D (2023) Parkin regulates amino acid homeostasis at mitochondria-lysosome (M/L) contact sites in Parkinson’s disease. Sci Adv 9:eadh3347")). Cholesterol: In NPC1-deficient cells, STARD3 is required for M-L contacts and for the buildup of lysosomal cholesterol in mitochondria (Höglinger et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR48 "Höglinger D, Burgoyne T, Sanchez-Heras E, Hartwig P, Colaco A, Newton J, Futter CE, Spiegel S, Platt FM, Eden ER (2019) NPC1 regulates ER contacts with endocytic organelles to mediate cholesterol egress. Nat Commun 10:4276")). Outside this setting, STARD3 has been studied mainly at ER–endosome contacts. Solid arrows show transfer demonstrated at M-L contact sites; dashed arrows show proposed or context-specific routes.

The M-L contacts were present at a significant amount of mitochondrial fission sites, indicated by areas positive for DRP1, highlighting the role of these membrane contact sites in mitochondrial dynamics (Wong et al, [2018](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR138 "Wong YC, Ysselstein D, Krainc D (2018) Mitochondria-lysosome contacts regulate mitochondrial fission via RAB7 GTP hydrolysis. Nature 554:382–386")). In addition, dysfunctions in mitochondrial fusion and fission events have been linked to the formation of mega mitochondria and donut-shaped mitochondria (Jenkins et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR52 "Jenkins BC, Neikirk K, Katti P, Claypool SM, Kirabo A, McReynolds MR, Hinton A Jr (2024) Mitochondria in disease: changes in shapes and dynamics. Trends Biochem Sci 49:346–360")). It has been suggested that these changes in shape could be to increase surface area for membrane contact sites with other organelles, as they often increase in diseased or stressed states (Jenkins et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR52 "Jenkins BC, Neikirk K, Katti P, Claypool SM, Kirabo A, McReynolds MR, Hinton A Jr (2024) Mitochondria in disease: changes in shapes and dynamics. Trends Biochem Sci 49:346–360")).

Emerging evidence suggests that mitochondria–lysosome membrane contacts are unlikely to represent a single molecular entity. While the RAB7–Fis1–TBC1D15 machinery regulates dynamic tethering and untethering events associated with mitochondrial fission, GDAP1 contributes to mitochondria–lysosome tethering through interaction with the lysosomal membrane protein LAMP1 (Cantarero et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR16 "Cantarero L, Juárez-Escoto E, Civera-Tregón A, Rodríguez-Sanz M, Roldán M, Benítez R, Hoenicka J, Palau F (2021) Mitochondria-lysosome membrane contacts are defective in GDAP1-related Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease. Hum Mol Genet 29:3589–3605")). More recent evidence further suggests that GDAP1-dependent membrane contacts constitute specialized redox-sensitive microdomains, raising the possibility that altered contact-site chemistry, in addition to altered contact abundance, contributes to GDAP1-associated axonopathy (Cantarero et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR17 "Cantarero L, Roldán M, Rodríguez-Sanz M, Mathison AJ, Olivares-Fuentes M, Díaz-Osorio Y, Pijuan J, Frías M, Urrutia R, Hoenicka J, Palau F (2026) GDAP1 orchestrates redox signaling at membrane contact sites to preserve axonal integrity in Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease. Neurobiol Dis 227:107512")). Whether GDAP1–LAMP1 and RAB7-dependent contacts represent molecularly distinct contact-site populations, partially overlapping machineries, or different regulatory states of the same membrane interface remains unknown. The transfer of contents is a pivotal role of membrane contact sites, and M-L membrane contact sites have been implicated in calcium and iron homeostasis, as both mitochondria and lysosomes are storage sites for these molecules. The lysosomal calcium efflux channel, transient receptor potential mucolipin (TRPML1), when mutated, results in a reduction of mitochondrial calcium efflux, a greater number of lysosomes in contact with mitochondria, and a longer duration in contacts (Peng et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR103 "Peng W, Wong YC, Krainc D (2020) Mitochondria-lysosome contacts regulate mitochondrial Ca2+ dynamics via lysosomal TRPML1. Proc Natl Acad Sci USA 117:19266–19275")). This calcium transfer is also mediated by the outer mitochondrial membrane protein VDAC1 and the IMM protein MCU (Peng et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR103 "Peng W, Wong YC, Krainc D (2020) Mitochondria-lysosome contacts regulate mitochondrial Ca2+ dynamics via lysosomal TRPML1. Proc Natl Acad Sci USA 117:19266–19275")). A complete loss of TRPML1 results in severe mitochondrial dysfunction, mitochondrial calcium overload, and an increase in M-L membrane contact sites (Clement et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR24 "Clement D, Szabo EK, Krokeide SZ, Wiiger MT, Vincenti M, Palacios D, Chang Y-T, Grimm C, Patel S, Stenmark H et al (2023) The lysosomal calcium channel TRPML1 maintains mitochondrial fitness in NK cells through interorganelle cross-talk. J Immunol 211:1348–1358")). The transfer of Fe 2+ from lysosomes to mitochondria has been shown to be dependent on the presence of the protein BDH2, which produces the iron-binding molecule 2,5-DHBA (Rizzollo et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR111 "Rizzollo F, Escamilla-Ayala A, Fattorelli N, Lysiak NB, More S, Hernández Varas P, Barazzuol L, Van den Haute C, Van Asselberghs J, Nittner D et al (2025) BDH2-driven lysosome-to-mitochondria iron transfer shapes ferroptosis vulnerability of the melanoma cell states. Nat Metab 7:1851–1870")), at the outer mitochondrial membrane. Furthermore, interactions between mitochondria and transferrin-enriched endosomes, termed “kiss and run”, have been shown to occur in epithelial and blood cells, and M-L contact site duration was increased if endosome iron release was blocked (Sheftel et al, [2007](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR118 "Sheftel AD, Zhang A-S, Brown C, Shirihai OS, Ponka P (2007) Direct interorganellar transfer of iron from endosome to mitochondrion. Blood 110:125–132"); Das et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR30 "Das A, Nag S, Mason AB, Barroso MM (2016) Endosome-mitochondria interactions are modulated by iron release from transferrin. J Cell Biol 214:831–845")). M-L contacts have also been implicated in the transfer of amino acids from lysosomes to mitochondria and is promoted by parkin stabilization of active RAB7 (Peng et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR102 "Peng W, Schröder LF, Song P, Wong YC, Krainc D (2023) Parkin regulates amino acid homeostasis at mitochondria-lysosome (M/L) contact sites in Parkinson’s disease. Sci Adv 9:eadh3347")).

Additionally, M-L contact sites have been implicated in cholesterol export from the lysosome to the mitochondria. NPC1-deficient cells contained lysosomes with high levels of cholesterol, suggesting that their increased contacts with mitochondria may be the cause of mitochondrial cholesterol accumulation in Niemann–Pick type C (NPC) (Höglinger et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR48 "Höglinger D, Burgoyne T, Sanchez-Heras E, Hartwig P, Colaco A, Newton J, Futter CE, Spiegel S, Platt FM, Eden ER (2019) NPC1 regulates ER contacts with endocytic organelles to mediate cholesterol egress. Nat Commun 10:4276")). The accumulation of cholesterol in the mitochondria of these cells was shown to be dependent on STARD3, a protein studied mostly in the context of cholesterol transport from the ER to the endosome (Charman et al, [2010](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR20 "Charman M, Kennedy BE, Osborne N, Karten B (2010) MLN64 mediates egress of cholesterol from endosomes to mitochondria in the absence of functional Niemann-Pick Type C1 protein. J Lipid Res 51:1023–1034")). STARD3 is required for M-L contacts in NPC1-deficient cells and mediates the transfer of cholesterol to mitochondria (Höglinger et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR48 "Höglinger D, Burgoyne T, Sanchez-Heras E, Hartwig P, Colaco A, Newton J, Futter CE, Spiegel S, Platt FM, Eden ER (2019) NPC1 regulates ER contacts with endocytic organelles to mediate cholesterol egress. Nat Commun 10:4276")). Posterior work showed cholesterol transfer from endo-lysosomes containing NPC2 to mitochondria (Juhl et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR55 "Juhl AD, Heegaard CW, Werner S, Schneider G, Krishnan K, Covey DF, Wüstner D (2021) Quantitative imaging of membrane contact sites for sterol transfer between endo-lysosomes and mitochondria in living cells. Sci Rep 11:8927")). Furthermore, recent studies show that during glucose refeeding after starvation, TM4SF5 became enriched at M-L contact sites and that this supported cholesterol export from the lysosome to the mitochondria, resulting in increased mitochondrial fission and decreased oxidative phosphorylation (Kim et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR58 "Kim JE, Park S-Y, Kwak C, Lee Y, Song D-G, Jung JW, Lee H, Shin E-A, Pinanga Y, Pyo K-H et al (2024) Glucose-mediated mitochondrial reprogramming by cholesterol export at TM4SF5-enriched mitochondria-lysosome contact sites. Cancer Commun 44:47–75")). The M-L contact sites observed in NPC1 also involve the protein HKDC1 (Pastore et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR101 "Pastore R, Stanley J, Kiraly S, Dubois C, Monfregola J, De Luca A, Guerra G, Skehel M, Ballabio A, Platt FM et al (2026) HKDC1 contributes to aberrant lysosome-mitochondria contact in Niemann-Pick disease type C. Preprint at bioRxiv 
                https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.06.01.729255
                
              ")), a protein with hexokinase activity (Zapater et al, [2022](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR146 "Zapater JL, Lednovich KR, Khan MW, Pusec CM, Layden BT (2022) Hexokinase domain-containing protein-1 in metabolic diseases and beyond. Trends Endocrinol Metab 33:72–84")) and whose expression is regulated transcriptionally by TFEB (Cui et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR27 "Cui M, Yamano K, Yamamoto K, Yamamoto-Imoto H, Minami S, Yamamoto T, Matsui S, Kaminishi T, Shima T, Ogura M et al (2024) HKDC1, a target of TFEB, is essential to maintain both mitochondrial and lysosomal homeostasis, preventing cellular senescence. Proc Natl Acad Sci USA 121:e2306454120")). These studies raise the tantalizing possibility that M-L contacts may have a role in sugar and cholesterol metabolism and homeostasis.

The occurrence of extreme contacts between mitochondria and lysosomes, in which mitochondria seem to envelop the lysosome, has been described in two conditions. The cells lacking NPC1 often show lysosomes sandwiched between very large mitochondria (Höglinger et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR48 "Höglinger D, Burgoyne T, Sanchez-Heras E, Hartwig P, Colaco A, Newton J, Futter CE, Spiegel S, Platt FM, Eden ER (2019) NPC1 regulates ER contacts with endocytic organelles to mediate cholesterol egress. Nat Commun 10:4276")), while cells under hypoxia present “megamitochondria” surrounding lysosomes (Hao et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR46 "Hao T, Yu J, Wu Z, Jiang J, Gong L, Wang B, Guo H, Zhao H, Lu B, Engelender S et al (2023) Hypoxia-reprogramed megamitochondrion contacts and engulfs lysosome to mediate mitochondrial self-digestion. Nat Commun 14:4105")). It remains to be determined if these megamitochondria-lysosome contacts are simply a large number of contacts over an extended membrane area or if they differ in terms of properties from the more canonical M-L contacts.

Altogether, these findings support the view that mitochondria–lysosome contact sites function as communication hubs where ion exchange, lipid transfer, redox homeostasis, and signaling converge to coordinate organelle behavior, rather than serving merely as physical points of membrane apposition. Importantly, it remains to be determined how acute and chronic mitochondrial, lysosomal, and metabolic stresses impact mitochondria–lysosome contact formation, stability, and dissociation.

### Mitochondria and lysosome-related organelles (LROs)

The principles governing communication between mitochondria and lysosomes also extend to LROs, although the functional outcomes differ substantially. LROs comprise a heterogeneous group of organelles derived from the endolysosomal system that have evolved specialized functions in distinct cell types, including melanosomes in melanocytes, lytic granules in immune cells, and platelet dense granules (Luzio et al, [2014](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR76 "Luzio JP, Hackmann Y, Dieckmann NMG, Griffiths GM (2014) The biogenesis of lysosomes and lysosome-related organelles. Cold Spring Harb Perspect Biol 6:a016840")). Like lysosomes, LROs receive cargo through the endosomal trafficking machinery, but their maturation requires additional pathways that confer organelle-specific identity and function (Luzio et al, [2014](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR76 "Luzio JP, Hackmann Y, Dieckmann NMG, Griffiths GM (2014) The biogenesis of lysosomes and lysosome-related organelles. Cold Spring Harb Perspect Biol 6:a016840")).

Melanosomes provide the best-characterized example of mitochondria-LRO communication. Initial studies demonstrated that mitochondria establish dynamic physical contacts with melanosomes, that these interactions increase during melanosome biogenesis, and that disrupting mitofusin 2 (MFN2) or mitochondrial ATP production impairs both contact formation and melanosome maturation (Daniele et al, [2014](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR29 "Daniele T, Hurbain I, Vago R, Casari G, Raposo G, Tacchetti C, Schiaffino MV (2014) Mitochondria and melanosomes establish physical contacts modulated by Mfn2 and involved in organelle biogenesis. Curr Biol 24:393–403")). More recently, the molecular basis of these contacts was elucidated by the identification of a tethering complex involving the ER Ca 2+ sensor STIM1 and MFN2, which promotes mitochondria-melanosome contacts and is required for efficient melanosome maturation (Shiiba et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR119 "Shiiba I, Ishikawa Y, Oshio H, Ito N, Yamaguchi F, Nagashima S, Ando H, Umezawa K, Miura Y, Araiso Y et al (2026) STIM1-Mitofusin2 interactions tether mitochondria and melanosome contacts that promote melanosome maturation. Nat Commun 17:3593")). These findings indicate that, similarly to mitochondria–lysosome contacts, direct membrane apposition between mitochondria and LROs serves as a platform for functional communication rather than simply reflecting organelle proximity.

Melanosomes are biosynthetic and secretory organelles, and mitochondria appear to regulate their growth and maturation rather than degradative capacity. Supporting this concept, the mitochondrial fission factor MFF was recently shown to bud from mitochondria and localize to melanosomes, where it promotes actin-dependent melanosome division and maturation. Loss of MFF results in enlarged, immature melanosomes, revealing an unexpected extramitochondrial function for a classical mitochondrial dynamics protein (Magalhães Rebelo et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR78 "Magalhães Rebelo AP, Maracani A, Greco S, Dal Bello F, Santorelli L, Gerdol M, Pallavicini A, Knedlik T, Schiavon S, Scorrano L et al (2026) MFF budding from mitochondria regulates melanosome size and maturation. Nat Commun 17:3932")). Interestingly, while MFF has been implicated in mitochondrial fission, a protein that is involved in mitochondrial fusion, MFN2, is also involved in mitochondria-melanosome contact sites that promote melanosome biogenesis (Daniele et al, [2014](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR29 "Daniele T, Hurbain I, Vago R, Casari G, Raposo G, Tacchetti C, Schiaffino MV (2014) Mitochondria and melanosomes establish physical contacts modulated by Mfn2 and involved in organelle biogenesis. Curr Biol 24:393–403")) and maturation (Shiiba et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR119 "Shiiba I, Ishikawa Y, Oshio H, Ito N, Yamaguchi F, Nagashima S, Ando H, Umezawa K, Miura Y, Araiso Y et al (2026) STIM1-Mitofusin2 interactions tether mitochondria and melanosome contacts that promote melanosome maturation. Nat Commun 17:3593")). MFN2 has roles in mediating mitochondria-endoplasmic reticulum contacts as well as mitochondrial outer membrane fusion (Naón et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR88 "Naón D, Hernández-Alvarez MI, Shinjo S, Wieczor M, Ivanova S, Martins de Brito O, Quintana A, Hidalgo J, Palacín M, Aparicio P et al (2023) Splice variants of mitofusin 2 shape the endoplasmic reticulum and tether it to mitochondria.
