Science 380:eadh9351"); Schrepfer and Scorrano, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR116 "Schrepfer E, Scorrano L (2016) Mitofusins, from mitochondria to metabolism. Mol Cell 61:683–694")), raising the possibility that mitochondrial dynamics are deeply involved in the regulation of melanosome homeostasis.

Likewise, phosphoinositide metabolism contributes to melanosome biogenesis through PIKfyve. Inhibition of PIKfyve blocks melanosome maturation and causes the accumulation of immature melanosomal intermediates (Liggins et al, [2018](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR72 "Liggins MC, Flesher JL, Jahid S, Vasudeva P, Eby V, Takasuga S, Sasaki J, Sasaki T, Boissy RE, Ganesan AK (2018) PIKfyve regulates melanosome biogenesis. PLoS Genet 14:e1007290")). Interestingly, PtdIns(3,5)P 2 also plays a central role in canonical lysosomes by regulating lysosomal ion homeostasis and TRPML1-dependent Ca 2+ signaling, suggesting that related lipid signaling modules have been adapted to support distinct organelle functions.

Although the best-studied LRO is the melanosome, recent work suggests that communication between mitochondria and LROs extends beyond pigment cells. During hepatocyte dedifferentiation, mitochondria interact with specialized LROs that mediate mitochondrial clearance independently of conventional lysosomal degradation, revealing an alternative pathway for organelle remodeling during cell fate transitions (Ma et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR77 "Ma X, Manley S, Qian H, Li Y, Zhang C, Li K, Ding B, Guo F, Chen A, Zhang X et al (2023) Mitochondria-lysosome-related organelles mediate mitochondrial clearance during cellular dedifferentiation. Cell Rep 42:113291")). This observation further expands the functional repertoire of mitochondria-LRO communication beyond organelle biogenesis.

Together, these studies suggest that mitochondria employ conserved mechanisms—including membrane contact sites, phosphoinositide signaling, and the repurposing of mitochondrial dynamics proteins—to communicate with both lysosomes and LROs. The principal difference lies in the biological outcome: communication with canonical lysosomes primarily maintains metabolic homeostasis, stress adaptation, and quality control, whereas communication with LROs supports the biogenesis, maturation, and specialized functions of cell type-specific organelles (Luzio et al, [2014](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR76 "Luzio JP, Hackmann Y, Dieckmann NMG, Griffiths GM (2014) The biogenesis of lysosomes and lysosome-related organelles. Cold Spring Harb Perspect Biol 6:a016840"); Shiiba et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR119 "Shiiba I, Ishikawa Y, Oshio H, Ito N, Yamaguchi F, Nagashima S, Ando H, Umezawa K, Miura Y, Araiso Y et al (2026) STIM1-Mitofusin2 interactions tether mitochondria and melanosome contacts that promote melanosome maturation. Nat Commun 17:3593")).

## Mitochondria lysosome crosstalk in disease

### Mitochondrial diseases

Primary mitochondrial diseases are frequently accompanied by secondary lysosomal abnormalities (Deus et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR34 "Deus CM, Yambire KF, Oliveira PJ, Raimundo N (2020) Mitochondria-lysosome crosstalk: from physiology to neurodegeneration. Trends Mol Med 26:71–88")). While coexistence alone does not establish a specific mechanism, the AMPK–TFEB and MCOLN1-dependent pathways discussed below indicate that lysosomal dysfunction can actively contribute to mitochondrial pathophysiology rather than merely reflecting downstream bioenergetic failure. Chronic respiratory chain deficiency, mtDNA depletion, and defects in mitochondrial dynamics impair lysosomal homeostasis (Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591"); Baixauli et al, [2015](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR6 "Baixauli F, Acín-Pérez R, Villarroya-Beltrí C, Mazzeo C, Nuñez-Andrade N, Gabandé-Rodriguez E, Ledesma MD, Blázquez A, Martin MA, Falcón-Pérez JM et al (2015) Mitochondrial respiration controls lysosomal function during inflammatory T cell responses. Cell Metab 22:485–498"); Agostini et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR2 "Agostini F, Pereyra L, Dale J, Yambire KF, Maglioni S, Schiavi A, Ventura N, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2024) Upregulation of cholesterol synthesis by lysosomal defects requires a functional mitochondrial respiratory chain. J Biol Chem 300:107403")), involving the decrease of lysosomal PtdIns(3,5)P 2, reducing lysosomal hydrolytic activity and altering lysosomal lipid composition. These changes compromise autophagic flux and limit mitochondrial quality control, thereby establishing a feed-forward cycle in which defective mitochondria progressively worsen lysosomal function.

Mechanistically, suppression of the AMPK–TFEB signaling axis appears to be a central feature of chronic mitochondrial disease. While acute mitochondrial stress activates AMPK and TFEB to stimulate organelle biogenesis, chronic respiratory chain dysfunction instead suppresses both pathways (Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591"); Viscomi et al, [2011](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR130 "Viscomi C, Bottani E, Civiletto G, Cerutti R, Moggio M, Fagiolari G, Schon EA, Lamperti C, Zeviani M (2011) In vivo correction of COX deficiency by activation of the AMPK/PGC-1α axis. Cell Metab 14:80–90")), preventing appropriate lysosomal adaptation. Restoring lysosomal function by stimulating TRPML1-dependent lysosomal Ca 2+ release rescues lysosomal acidification, hydrolytic capacity, and mitochondrial physiology in cellular and animal models of mitochondrial disease (Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591"); Agostini et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR2 "Agostini F, Pereyra L, Dale J, Yambire KF, Maglioni S, Schiavi A, Ventura N, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2024) Upregulation of cholesterol synthesis by lysosomal defects requires a functional mitochondrial respiratory chain. J Biol Chem 300:107403")). These findings identify lysosomal signaling as a therapeutic entry point for disorders that originate from primary mitochondrial dysfunction. Further illustrating this principle in a genetically defined disease, mutations in FBXL4 cause mtDNA depletion syndrome by driving excessive NIX/BNIP3-mediated mitophagy that depletes otherwise functional mitochondria, rather than through impaired bioenergetics alone (Chen et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR22 "Chen Y, Jiao D, Liu Y, Xu X, Wang Y, Luo X, Saiyin H, Li Y, Gao K, Chen Y, Zhao S-M, Ma L, Wang C (2023) FBXL4 mutations cause excessive mitophagy via BNIP3/BNIP3L accumulation leading to mitochondrial DNA depletion syndrome. Cell Death Differ 30:2351–2363")). Pharmacological AMPK activation has recently emerged as a candidate therapeutic strategy in this context (Bishnu et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR9 "Bishnu A, Zapata-Muñoz J, Taylor RW, Sakamoto K, Ganley IG (2026) Activation of AMPK as a therapeutic strategy for FBXL4-related mitochondrial DNA depletion syndrome. EMBO Mol Med 18:3109–3136")), reinforcing AMPK signaling as a therapeutic target across genetically diverse forms of mitochondrial disease.

These observations illustrate that primary mitochondrial disorders are not solely diseases of impaired bioenergetics, but also diseases of defective inter-organelle communication. A detailed overview of mitochondria–lysosome crosstalk in mitochondrial diseases is presented in Table[1](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#Tab1). Therapeutic strategies that restore lysosomal signaling and communication pathways may therefore prove broadly applicable across genetically diverse mitochondrial diseases.

**Table 1 Mitochondria–lysosome crosstalk in mitochondrial diseases.**

[Full size table](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9/tables/1)

### Lysosomal storage diseases

Lysosomal storage diseases (LSDs) provide compelling evidence that primary lysosomal dysfunction profoundly remodels mitochondrial biology. Beyond the expected impairment of autophagic degradation, lysosomal defects directly alter mitochondrial metabolism, organelle dynamics, and stress signaling. A consistent feature across several lipid storage disorders is the repression of transcriptional programs controlling mitochondrial biogenesis (Yambire et al, [2019a](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR141 "Yambire KF, Fernandez-Mosquera L, Steinfeld R, Mühle C, Ikonen E, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019a) Mitochondrial biogenesis is transcriptionally repressed in lysosomal lipid storage diseases. eLife 8:e39598")), suggesting that cells adapt to defective lysosomal degradation by limiting the production of new mitochondria. Although this response may reduce the burden on impaired degradative pathways, it also compromises cellular bioenergetic capacity and contributes to disease progression.

Among the best-characterized examples is GBA1 deficiency. Mutations in GBA1, encoding the lysosomal enzyme glucocerebrosidase, represent one of the strongest genetic risk factors for Parkinson’s disease and produce widespread alterations in mitochondrial morphology, respiratory function, and quality control (Gegg and Schapira, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR41 "Gegg ME, Schapira AHV (2016) Mitochondrial dysfunction associated with glucocerebrosidase deficiency. Neurobiol Dis 90:43–50")). GBA1 deficiency was also shown to disrupt the dynamics of mitochondria–lysosome contact sites, resulting in excessive organelle tethering, impaired mitochondrial function, and defective contact-site resolution (Kim et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR59 "Kim S, Wong YC, Gao F, Krainc D (2021) Dysregulation of mitochondria-lysosome contacts by GBA1 dysfunction in dopaminergic neuronal models of Parkinson’s disease. Nat Commun 12:1807")). These findings demonstrate that lysosomal dysfunction perturbs mitochondrial physiology through both degradative and non-degradative mechanisms.

Lysosomal ion homeostasis also plays a central role in regulating mitochondrial function. Genetic or pharmacological disruption of TRPML1 signaling alters mitochondrial Ca 2+ homeostasis, mitochondrial quality control, and cellular survival across multiple disease models (Peng et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR103 "Peng W, Wong YC, Krainc D (2020) Mitochondria-lysosome contacts regulate mitochondrial Ca2+ dynamics via lysosomal TRPML1. Proc Natl Acad Sci USA 117:19266–19275"); Siow et al, [2022](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR120 "Siow WX, Kabiri Y, Tang R, Chao Y-K, Plesch E, Eberhagen C, Flenkenthaler F, Fröhlich T, Bracher F, Grimm C et al (2022) Lysosomal TRPML1 regulates mitochondrial function in hepatocellular carcinoma cells. J Cell Sci 135:jcs259455"); Clement et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR24 "Clement D, Szabo EK, Krokeide SZ, Wiiger MT, Vincenti M, Palacios D, Chang Y-T, Grimm C, Patel S, Stenmark H et al (2023) The lysosomal calcium channel TRPML1 maintains mitochondrial fitness in NK cells through interorganelle cross-talk. J Immunol 211:1348–1358")). Likewise, defects in lysosomal cholesterol handling, such as those occurring in Niemann–Pick disease type C, increase M-L contacts and promote cholesterol accumulation within mitochondria, thereby compromising mitochondrial metabolism (Höglinger et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR48 "Höglinger D, Burgoyne T, Sanchez-Heras E, Hartwig P, Colaco A, Newton J, Futter CE, Spiegel S, Platt FM, Eden ER (2019) NPC1 regulates ER contacts with endocytic organelles to mediate cholesterol egress. Nat Commun 10:4276"); Juhl et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR55 "Juhl AD, Heegaard CW, Werner S, Schneider G, Krishnan K, Covey DF, Wüstner D (2021) Quantitative imaging of membrane contact sites for sterol transfer between endo-lysosomes and mitochondria in living cells. Sci Rep 11:8927")). Together, these studies indicate that abnormalities in lysosomal lipid and ion homeostasis propagate directly to mitochondria through multiple complementary signaling pathways.

Alterations in lysosomal physiology extend beyond defects in degradative capacity. Chronic inhibition of the lysosomal v-ATPase causes mitochondrial fragmentation, respiratory impairment, and increased mitochondrial superoxide production through functional iron deficiency (Yambire et al, [2019b](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR142 "Yambire KF, Rostosky C, Watanabe T, Pacheu-Grau D, Torres-Odio S, Sanchez-Guerrero A, Senderovich O, Meyron-Holtz EG, Milosevic I, Frahm J et al (2019b) Impaired lysosomal acidification triggers iron deficiency and inflammation in vivo. eLife 8:e51031")). Other lysosomal defects have also been shown to trigger mitochondrial fragmentation (Leal et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR66 "Leal AF, Saikia S, Khan SA, Tomatsu S (2025) Three-dimensional human mucopolysaccharidosis IVA chondrocyte culture reveals significant impairments in the lysosomal-mitochondrial crosstalk. Sci Rep 15:34140")). Lysosomal acidification is also required for efficient activation of the mitochondrial unfolded protein response during mitochondrial stress (Li et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR70 "Li TY, Wang Q, Gao AW, Li X, Sun Y, Mottis A, Shong M, Auwerx J (2023) Lysosomes mediate the mitochondrial UPR via mTORC1-dependent ATF4 phosphorylation. Cell Discov 9:92")), implying that lysosome function is necessary for the ability of mitochondria to respond to stress. Intriguingly, pharmacological or genetic inhibition of the v-ATPase also increases mitochondrial membrane potential, an effect that has recently been exploited to improve mitochondrial bioenergetics in cellular models of mitochondrial disease, although the underlying mechanisms remain unknown (Monteuuis et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR86 "Monteuuis G, Awadhpersad R, van der Kolk D, Singh SK, Nyman TA, Malyutina A, Zamboni N, Moisio K, Juutila J, Hietakangas V et al (2025) Vacuolar-type H+-ATPase-mediated extra-organellar buffering resolves mitochondrial dysfunction. Nat Commun 17:67")).

Another pathway linking lysosomal dysfunction to mitochondrial disease involves nutrient sensing through mTORC1. Although activation of lysosomal mTORC1 supports mitochondrial metabolism under physiological conditions (Norambuena et al, [2018](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR96 "Norambuena A, Wallrabe H, Cao R, Wang DB, Silva A, Svindrych Z, Periasamy A, Hu S, Tanzi RE, Kim DY et al (2018) A novel lysosome-to-mitochondria signaling pathway disrupted by amyloid-β oligomers. EMBO J 37:e100241")), partial inhibition of mTORC1 has demonstrated therapeutic benefit in several models of mitochondrial encephalopathy and myopathy (Johnson et al, [2013](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR54 "Johnson SC, Yanos ME, Kayser E-B, Quintana A, Sangesland M, Castanza A, Uhde L, Hui J, Wall VZ, Gagnidze A et al (2013) mTOR inhibition alleviates mitochondrial disease in a mouse model of Leigh syndrome. Science 342:1524–1528"); Khan et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR57 "Khan NA, Nikkanen J, Yatsuga S, Jackson C, Wang L, Pradhan S, Kivelä R, Pessia A, Velagapudi V, Suomalainen A (2017) mTORC1 regulates mitochondrial integrated stress response and mitochondrial myopathy progression. Cell Metab 26:419–428.e5"); Lehmer and Zoncu, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR67 "Lehmer M, Zoncu R (2024) mTORC1 signaling inhibition modulates mitochondrial function in frataxin deficiency. Preprint at bioRxiv 
                https://doi.org/10.1101/2024.08.06.606942
                
              ")). Together with studies demonstrating beneficial effects of AMPK activation in mitochondrial myopathies (Fernandez-Mosquera et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR40 "Fernandez-Mosquera L, Yambire KF, Couto R, Pereyra L, Pabis K, Ponsford AH, Diogo CV, Stagi M, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2019) Mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency inhibits lysosomal hydrolysis. Autophagy 15:1572–1591"); Viscomi et al, [2011](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR130 "Viscomi C, Bottani E, Civiletto G, Cerutti R, Moggio M, Fagiolari G, Schon EA, Lamperti C, Zeviani M (2011) In vivo correction of COX deficiency by activation of the AMPK/PGC-1α axis. Cell Metab 14:80–90")), these findings suggest that successful therapeutic intervention may depend on restoring the balance between AMPK and mTORC1 signaling rather than simply enhancing or suppressing either pathway alone. The tissue specificity of mitochondrial diseases is also likely to influence this balance, as AMPK activation appears beneficial in skeletal muscle but may have adverse consequences in neuronal tissues (McKay et al, [2015](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR81 "McKay SE, Yan W, Nouws J, Thormann MJ, Raimundo N, Khan A, Santos-Sacchi J, Song L, Shadel GS (2015) Auditory pathology in a transgenic mtTFB1 mouse model of mitochondrial deafness. Am J Pathol 185:3132–3140"); Raimundo et al, [2012](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR109 "Raimundo N, Song L, Shutt TE, McKay SE, Cotney J, Guan M-X, Gilliland TC, Hohuan D, Santos-Sacchi J, Shadel GS (2012) Mitochondrial stress engages E2F1 apoptotic signaling to cause deafness. Cell 148:716–726")).

The recognition that mitochondrial dysfunction is a major secondary consequence of lysosomal disease has important therapeutic implications. Mitochondria–lysosome crosstalk is explored in detail in the context of lysosomal storage diseases in Table[2](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#Tab2). Interventions that restore lysosomal signaling, normalize Ca 2+ homeostasis or improve mitochondria–lysosome communication may simultaneously correct lysosomal pathology and mitochondrial dysfunction. Viewed through the lens of organelle communication, lysosomal storage diseases extend beyond defects in intracellular degradation. Instead, they illustrate how perturbation of one component of the communication network propagates across organelles, leading to secondary mitochondrial dysfunction and widespread metabolic remodeling. Consequently, targeting the crosstalk between these organelles, rather than either organelle in isolation, may provide broader therapeutic benefit across genetically diverse lysosomal disorders.

**Table 2 Mitochondria–lysosome crosstalk in lysosomal storage diseases.**

[Full size table](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9/tables/2)

### Cardiac diseases

In the field of cardiovascular diseases, functional interactions between mitochondria and lysosomes have drawn significant attention in past years (see Table[3](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#Tab3)). The heart is an organ with high energy demands, requiring robust mitochondrial homeostasis to sustain cardiac function; thus, mitochondrial dysfunction has been implicated as a key driver of many heart diseases (Peoples et al, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR104 "Peoples JN, Saraf A, Ghazal N, Pham TT, Kwong JQ (2019) Mitochondrial dysfunction and oxidative stress in heart disease. Exp Mol Med 51:1–13")). Similarly, cardiomyopathy, a disease affecting the heart muscle, has been increasingly associated with impaired lysosomal function. Diseases affecting lysosomal function, such as LSDs, manifest with cardiac complications, i.e., hypertrophic or dilated cardiomyopathy. Along with these, several coronary conditions have been associated with either excessive or insufficient lysosomal functions (Chi et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR23 "Chi C, Riching AS, Song K (2020) Lysosomal abnormalities in cardiovascular disease. Int J Mol Sci 21:811")). Although these co-occurring organellar defects highlight that mitochondrial and lysosomal health are broadly interdependent (Fernández-Mosquera et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR39 "Fernández-Mosquera L, Diogo CV, Yambire KF, Santos GL, Luna Sánchez M, Bénit P, Rustin P, Lopez LC, Milosevic I, Raimundo N (2017) Acute and chronic mitochondrial respiratory chain deficiency differentially regulate lysosomal biogenesis. Sci Rep 7:45076")), whether this interdependence relies on direct physical contact sites or downstream cellular stress responses remains insufficiently defined in many cardiac disease settings.

**Table 3 Mitochondria–lysosome crosstalk in cardiovascular disease.**

[Full size table](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9/tables/3)

Danon disease is a rare X-linked lysosomal storage disorder caused by a mutation in the _LAMP2_ gene and manifested by defective autophagosome–lysosome fusion and autophagic flux (Rowland et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR112 "Rowland TJ, Sweet ME, Mestroni L, Taylor MRG (2016) Danon disease - dysregulation of autophagy in a multisystem disorder with cardiomyopathy. J Cell Sci 129:2135–2143")). This is an excellent example of how primary lysosomal disruption can secondarily impair mitochondrial clearance. Mechanistically, LAMP2 loss impairs lysosomal acidification and autophagosome–lysosome fusion, directly blocking mitophagic clearance of mitochondria; this defined mechanistic link—rather than mere coexistence of organelle abnormalities—drives the progressive accumulation of dysfunctional mitochondria (Nascimbeni et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR91 "Nascimbeni AC, Fanin M, Angelini C, Sandri M (2017) Autophagy dysregulation in Danon disease. Cell Death Dis 8:e2565"); Rowland et al, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR112 "Rowland TJ, Sweet ME, Mestroni L, Taylor MRG (2016) Danon disease - dysregulation of autophagy in a multisystem disorder with cardiomyopathy. J Cell Sci 129:2135–2143")). Chronic defects in these pathways ultimately lead to cardiomyocyte apoptosis and heart failure. Human induced pluripotent stem cell-derived cardiomyocyte (hiPSC-CM) models of Danon disease display marked accumulation of fragmented mitochondria, stalled autophagosomes, and heightened mitochondrial reactive oxygen species (ROS) (Hashem et al, [2015](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR47 "Hashem SI, Perry CN, Bauer M, Han S, Clegg SD, Ouyang K, Deacon DC, Spinharney M, Panopoulos AD, Izpisua Belmonte JC et al (2015) Brief report: oxidative stress mediates cardiomyocyte apoptosis in a human model of Danon disease and heart failure. Stem Cells 33:2343–2350")). Treatment with the antioxidant N-acetylcysteine scavenges the free radicals and rescues cardiomyocyte viability in this hiPSC-CM model (Hashem et al, [2015](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR47 "Hashem SI, Perry CN, Bauer M, Han S, Clegg SD, Ouyang K, Deacon DC, Spinharney M, Panopoulos AD, Izpisua Belmonte JC et al (2015) Brief report: oxidative stress mediates cardiomyocyte apoptosis in a human model of Danon disease and heart failure. Stem Cells 33:2343–2350")). While these findings demonstrate that primary lysosomal dysfunction secondarily impacts mitochondrial homeostasis, direct physical contact sites or dedicated bidirectional signaling mechanisms between the two organelles in Danon disease are yet to be resolved.

In contrast, more direct interaction through M-L contact sites has been implicated in acute cardiac tissue injury models. Upon acute myocardial infarction (MI), overexpression of TBC1D15 improves myocardial morphology and cardiac systolic function through direct manipulation of M-L contact dynamics (Yu et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR145 "Yu W, Sun S, Xu H, Li C, Ren J, Zhang Y (2020) TBC1D15/RAB7-regulated mitochondria-lysosome interaction confers cardioprotection against acute myocardial infarction-induced cardiac injury. Theranostics 10:11244–11263")). Specifically, following an acute MI, M-L contact distances were decreased, and duration was increased, and these structural alterations can be rescued with TBC1D15 overexpression (Yu et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR145 "Yu W, Sun S, Xu H, Li C, Ren J, Zhang Y (2020) TBC1D15/RAB7-regulated mitochondria-lysosome interaction confers cardioprotection against acute myocardial infarction-induced cardiac injury. Theranostics 10:11244–11263")). Mechanistically, TBC1D15 recruits DRP1 to M-L contact sites to drive asymmetrical mitochondrial fission, thereby maintaining organelle quality control (Sun et al, [2022](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR124 "Sun S, Yu W, Xu H, Li C, Zou R, Wu NN, Wang L, Ge J, Ren J, Zhang Y (2022) TBC1D15-Drp1 interaction-mediated mitochondrial homeostasis confers cardioprotection against myocardial ischemia/reperfusion injury. Metabolism 134:155239")). These studies provide evidence that altered M-L tethering has functional consequences in the heart and suggest that targeted manipulation of these contact sites may represent a therapeutic avenue for ischemic heart disease.

Beyond contact sites, crosstalk between mitochondrial outer membrane remodeling and lysosomal iron transfer has emerged as a potential axis in heart failure. Stress-induced OPA1 processing from long-OPA1 to short-OPA1 results in increased mitochondrial fragmentation leading to cardiomyopathy and heart failure (Wai et al, [2015](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR132 "Wai T, García-Prieto J, Baker MJ, Merkwirth C, Benit P, Rustin P, Rupérez FJ, Barbas C, Ibañez B, Langer T (2015) Imbalanced OPA1 processing and mitochondrial fragmentation cause heart failure in mice. Science 350:aad0116")). However, this has been attributed to a metabolic switch in fuel utilization from fatty acid oxidation to glucose use. A more recent study by Ahola et al showed that impaired OPA1 processing aggravated mitochondrial cardiopathy in OXPHOS-deficient cardiomyocytes (Ahola et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR3 "Ahola S, Pazurek LA, Mayer F, Lampe P, Hermans S, Becker L, Amarie OV, Fuchs H, Gailus-Durner V, de Angelis MH et al (2024) Opa1 processing is dispensable in mouse development but is protective in mitochondrial cardiomyopathy. Sci Adv 10:eadp0443")). In addition, deregulated autophagy, stalled mitophagy, and prolonged mito-ER contacts were observed in these cells with impaired OPA1 processing.

Concurrently, OPA1 has been shown to promote ferroptosis in mouse and human models via its GTPase activity, independent of its role in mitochondrial fusion (Liang et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR71 "Liang FG, Zandkarimi F, Lee J, Axelrod JL, Pekson R, Yoon Y, Stockwell BR, Kitsis RN (2024) OPA1 promotes ferroptosis by augmenting mitochondrial ROS and suppressing an integrated stress response. Mol Cell 84:3098–3114.e6")). In addition, OPA1 deficiency confers ferroptosis-resistance in these cells. As ferroptosis is regulated by lysosomal functions, these findings raise the possibility of an underlying functional link between OPA1 activity and lysosomal iron dynamics. Under basal conditions, iron is efficiently shuttled between mitochondria and lysosomes. However, during stress, mitochondrial dysfunction can sensitize cells to ferroptosis by increasing oxidative stress (Deng et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR33 "Deng R, Fu L, Liang H, Ai X, Liu F, Li N, Wu L, Li S, Yang X, Lin Y et al (2025) Inhibition of mitochondrial complex I induces mitochondrial ferroptosis by regulating CoQH2 levels in cancer. Cell Death Dis 16:254")), and by decreasing the efficiency of Fe 2+ import from lysosomes, via TRPML1-VDAC2/3 (Li et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR69 "Li J, Feng R, Yang W, Liang P, Qiu T, Zhang J, Sun X, Li Q, Yang G, Yao X (2024) Lysosomal iron accumulation and subsequent lysosomes-mitochondria iron transmission mediate PFOS-induced hepatocyte ferroptosis. Ecotoxicol Environ Saf 284:116890")) or via BDH2 (Rizzollo et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR111 "Rizzollo F, Escamilla-Ayala A, Fattorelli N, Lysiak NB, More S, Hernández Varas P, Barazzuol L, Van den Haute C, Van Asselberghs J, Nittner D et al (2025) BDH2-driven lysosome-to-mitochondria iron transfer shapes ferroptosis vulnerability of the melanoma cell states. Nat Metab 7:1851–1870")). Impaired export from lysosomes leads to Fe 2+ accumulation in lysosomes, which upon cytosolic release, triggers Fenton chemistry-mediated lipid peroxidation, and ferroptotic cardiomyocyte death (Rizzollo et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR111 "Rizzollo F, Escamilla-Ayala A, Fattorelli N, Lysiak NB, More S, Hernández Varas P, Barazzuol L, Van den Haute C, Van Asselberghs J, Nittner D et al (2025) BDH2-driven lysosome-to-mitochondria iron transfer shapes ferroptosis vulnerability of the melanoma cell states. Nat Metab 7:1851–1870")). Whether OPA1 directly modulates physical M-L tethering or indirectly influences inter-organellar iron exchange remains an open question requiring further investigation.

### Neurodegeneration

Neurodegeneration refers to the progressive loss of neuron functions and associated damage, culminating in neuronal cell death. This deviation from neuronal homeostasis is the primary cause of many neurodegenerative diseases, such as Alzheimer’s disease (AD), Parkinson’s disease (PD), amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS), and Huntington’s disease (HD). Intricately regulated organelle dynamics is essential for neuronal homeostasis, i.e., impairment in mitochondria and lysosomal functions may result in neurological disorders (see Table[4](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#Tab4)). As discussed previously, these two organelles interact with each other via degradative (e.g., mitophagy) and non-degradative (e.g., contact site formation) pathways to maintain cellular health. Past studies have largely focused on the role of mitophagy in neurodegeneration (Antico et al, [2025](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR5 "Antico O, Thompson PW, Hertz NT, Muqit MMK, Parton LE (2025) Targeting mitophagy in neurodegenerative diseases. Nat Rev Drug Discov 24:276–299"); Cen et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR18 "Cen X, Zhang M, Zhou M, Ye L, Xia H (2021) Mitophagy regulates neurodegenerative diseases. Cells 10:1876"); Jetto et al, [2022](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR53 "Jetto CT, Nambiar A, Manjithaya R (2022) Mitophagy and neurodegeneration: between the knowns and the unknowns. Front Cell Dev Biol 10:837337")); however, recently mitochondria–lysosomal contact sites are of particular focus in orchestrating some of the pathologies associated with these diseases. Here, we will review some of the most common neurodegenerative diseases and discuss how M-L contacts may be involved in disease progression.

**Table 4 Mitochondria–lysosome crosstalk in neurodegeneration.**

[Full size table](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9/tables/4)

Parkinson’s disease (PD) exemplifies how disruption of mitochondria–lysosome communication contributes to neurodegeneration. While defects in PINK1/Parkin-mediated mitophagy have long dominated the field, increasing evidence indicates that perturbations in inter-organelle communication independently contribute to dopaminergic neuron loss. Rather than representing isolated defects of mitochondria or lysosomes, PD is increasingly viewed as a failure of the integrated network that coordinates organelle homeostasis (Coukos and Krainc, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR26 "Coukos R, Krainc D (2024) Key genes and convergent pathogenic mechanisms in Parkinson disease. Nat Rev Neurosci 25:393–413")). One of the strongest examples is provided by mutations in _GBA1_, the most common genetic risk factor for PD. Beyond causing lysosomal dysfunction, _GBA1_ deficiency profoundly alters mitochondrial morphology and respiration (Gegg and Schapira, [2016](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR41 "Gegg ME, Schapira AHV (2016) Mitochondrial dysfunction associated with glucocerebrosidase deficiency. Neurobiol Dis 90:43–50")). Importantly, _GBA1_-mutant dopaminergic neurons display prolonged mitochondria–lysosome tethering due to defective contact-site resolution, leading to mitochondrial dysfunction that can be rescued by restoring physiological contact dynamics through TBC1D15 overexpression (Kim et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR59 "Kim S, Wong YC, Gao F, Krainc D (2021) Dysregulation of mitochondria-lysosome contacts by GBA1 dysfunction in dopaminergic neuronal models of Parkinson’s disease. Nat Commun 12:1807")). In contrast, loss of M-L contact sites has also been documented in a PD-related protein α-synuclein overexpression model. α-synuclein overexpression impairs M-L contact site formation, disrupting efficient mitochondrial Ca 2+ uptake and buffering from lysosomes (Giamogante et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR42 "Giamogante F, Barazzuol L, Maiorca F, Poggio E, Esposito A, Masato A, Napolitano G, Vagnoni A, Calì T, Brini M (2024) A SPLICS reporter reveals [Formula: see text]-synuclein regulation of lysosome-mitochondria contacts which affects TFEB nuclear translocation. Nat Commun 15:1516")). As lysosomal Ca 2+ release through TRPML1 regulates mitochondrial Ca 2+ homeostasis at contact sites (Peng et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR103 "Peng W, Wong YC, Krainc D (2020) Mitochondria-lysosome contacts regulate mitochondrial Ca2+ dynamics via lysosomal TRPML1. Proc Natl Acad Sci USA 117:19266–19275")), pharmacological activation of TRPML1 restores mitochondrial and lysosomal function in experimental models of PD (Pollmanns et al, [2022](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR107 "Pollmanns MR, Beer J, Rosignol I, Rodriguez-Muela N, Falkenburger BH, Dinter E (2022) Activated endolysosomal cation channel TRPML1 facilitates maturation of α-synuclein-containing autophagosomes. Front Cell Neurosci 16:861202")). Hence, any deviation from physiological M-L contact dynamics, whether characterized by prolonged tethering or insufficient contact, can trigger PD-associated phenotypes.

Finally, the M-L contact sites are also perturbed in PD neurons (Kim et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR59 "Kim S, Wong YC, Gao F, Krainc D (2021) Dysregulation of mitochondria-lysosome contacts by GBA1 dysfunction in dopaminergic neuronal models of Parkinson’s disease. Nat Commun 12:1807")). Parkin-dependent mechanisms provide further links between mitochondrial and lysosomal quality control in PD. Mutations in MID51 have also been associated with PD; MID51 and FIS1 promote each other’s oligomerization, and a MID51 mutant that prevents this oligomerization disrupts lysosomal tethering events (Wong et al, [2022](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR136 "Wong YC, Kim S, Cisneros J, Molakal CG, Song P, Lubbe SJ, Krainc D (2022) Mid51/Fis1 mitochondrial oligomerization complex drives lysosomal untethering and network dynamics. J Cell Biol 221:e202206140")), connecting the mitochondrial fission machinery to contact-site regulation. Separately, PD-associated Parkin mutants fail to support MDV formation, contributing to the accumulation of damaged mitochondrial components (McLelland et al, [2014](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR83 "McLelland G-L, Soubannier V, Chen CX, McBride HM, Fon EA (2014) Parkin and PINK1 function in a vesicular trafficking pathway regulating mitochondrial quality control. EMBO J 33:282–295")). More broadly, loss of Parkin-mediated mitophagy has long been linked to PD pathogenesis (Kitada et al, [1998](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR60 "Kitada T, Asakawa S, Hattori N, Matsumine H, Yamamura Y, Minoshima S, Yokochi M, Mizuno Y, Shimizu N (1998) Mutations in the parkin gene cause autosomal recessive juvenile parkinsonism. Nature 392:605–608")), and aged Parkin knockout mice display locomotor dysfunction, neuronal loss, and abnormal, fragmented mitochondria in dopaminergic neurons (Noda et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR95 "Noda S, Sato S, Fukuda T, Tada N, Uchiyama Y, Tanaka K, Hattori N (2020) Loss of Parkin contributes to mitochondrial turnover and dopaminergic neuronal loss in aged mice. Neurobiol Dis 136:104717")).

Together, these findings support a model in which PD arises, at least in part, from the progressive breakdown of bidirectional communication between mitochondria and lysosomes. Altered contact dynamics, defective lysosomal Ca 2+ signaling, and impaired vesicular trafficking reinforce mitochondrial dysfunction and lysosomal failure, establishing a self-perpetuating cycle that ultimately compromises neuronal survival. These findings suggest that Parkinson’s disease reflects the progressive destabilization of a mitochondria–lysosome communication network. Altered contact dynamics, defective lysosomal signaling, and impaired vesicular communication reinforce one another, providing a conceptual framework that extends beyond defects in mitophagy alone.

Alzheimer’s disease (AD) manifests from toxicity conferred by the misfolding and aggregation of microtubule-associated protein tau and amyloid-β peptides cleaved from the amyloid precursor protein (APP) (Abdulkhaliq et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR1 "Abdulkhaliq AA, Kim B, Almoghrabi YM, Khan J, Ajoolabady A, Ren J, Bahijri S, Tuomilehto J, Borai A, Pratico D (2026) Amyloid-β and Tau in Alzheimer’s disease: pathogenesis, mechanisms, and interplay. Cell Death Dis 17:21")). To resolve toxic protein aggregates, cells have various clearance mechanisms, such as proteasomal activity, autophagy, and secretory extracellular vesicles containing misfolded protein aggregates. However, loss of lysosomal activity is one of the major pathological events observed in AD disease progression. Lysosomes become enlarged with a decreased lysosomal hydrolase (i.e., cathepsin D) activity, thereby promoting further accumulation of toxic aggregates and worsening cellular health (Drobny et al, [2022](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR38 "Drobny A, Prieto Huarcaya S, Dobert J, Kluge A, Bunk J, Schlothauer T, Zunke F (2022) The role of lysosomal cathepsins in neurodegeneration: mechanistic insights, diagnostic potential and therapeutic approaches. Biochim Biophys Acta Mol Cell Res 1869:119243")). In addition to lysosomal degradation pathways, a mitochondria-mediated proteostasis mechanism MAGIC (mitochondria as guardian in cytosol) has also been described in yeast, along with its potential existence in human cells (Ruan et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR113 "Ruan L, Zhou C, Jin E, Kucharavy A, Zhang Y, Wen Z, Florens L, Li R (2017) Cytosolic proteostasis through importing of misfolded proteins into mitochondria. Nature 543:443–446")). A study by Ruan et al showed that cytosolic protein aggregates formed due to heat shock are imported into mitochondria for degradation in yeast cells via the TOM import machinery. Interestingly, Petersen et al previously showed that amyloid-β peptides localize to human brain mitochondria and are imported via the TOM complex (Hansson Petersen et al, [2008](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR45 "Hansson Petersen CA, Alikhani N, Behbahani H, Wiehager B, Pavlov PF, Alafuzoff I, Leinonen V, Ito A, Winblad B, Glaser E et al (2008) The amyloid beta-peptide is imported into mitochondria via the TOM import machinery and localized to mitochondrial cristae. Proc Natl Acad Sci USA 105:13145–13150")). It is still unclear why mitochondria take up these toxic proteins and whether these proteins are being degraded by mitochondrial proteolysis enzymes, as was shown in yeast cells by Ruan et al. It would also be interesting to investigate if MAGIC is an alternative mechanism for cells to compensate for the loss of lysosomal degradation in AD pathologies.

A novel lysosome to mitochondria signaling pathway was described by Norambuena et al, where they coined the term Nutrient-induced Mitochondrial Activity (NiMA) and investigated this phenomenon in the context of AD (Norambuena et al, [2018](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR96 "Norambuena A, Wallrabe H, Cao R, Wang DB, Silva A, Svindrych Z, Periasamy A, Hu S, Tanzi RE, Kim DY et al (2018) A novel lysosome-to-mitochondria signaling pathway disrupted by amyloid-β oligomers. EMBO J 37:e100241")). Here, they showed that insulin or amino acids trigger NiMA by activating lysosomal mTORC1, not mitochondrial mTORC1, in neuronal cells. In AD neurons, amyloid-β oligomers (AβOs), soluble building blocks of poorly soluble amyloid plaques, stimulate Tau-dependent mTORC1 translocation from lysosomes to plasma membrane (PM). PM-localized mTORC1 activation leads to neuron cell death in AD by triggering cell cycle re-entry, a frequent phenomenon in AD pathogenesis. Parallelly, as a consequence of this pathway, AβOs inhibit lysosomal mTORC1 activation and block NiMA. This study provides an example of how defective lysosome-mitochondria crosstalk can contribute to pathogenic progression in AD. Functional defects associated with AD include disrupted lipid metabolism (Di Paolo and Kim, [2011](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR35 "Di Paolo G, Kim T-W (2011) Linking lipids to Alzheimer’s disease: cholesterol and beyond. Nat Rev Neurosci 12:284–296"); Yin, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR143 "Yin F (2023) Lipid metabolism and Alzheimer’s disease: clinical evidence, mechanistic link and therapeutic promise. FEBS J 290:1420–1453")), improper Ca 2+ shuttling (Alzheimer’s Association Calcium Hypothesis Workgroup, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR4 "Alzheimer’s Association Calcium Hypothesis Workgroup (2017) Calcium hypothesis of Alzheimer’s disease and brain aging: a framework for integrating new evidence into a comprehensive theory of pathogenesis. Alzheimers Dement 13:178–182.e17")), impaired glucose metabolism and energy generation (Butterfield and Halliwell, [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR13 "Butterfield DA, Halliwell B (2019) Oxidative stress, dysfunctional glucose metabolism and Alzheimer disease. Nat Rev Neurosci 20:148–160")). Given the role of direct M-L contact site formation in regulating these pathways, it can be speculated that M-L contacts may be responsible for driving some of these phenotypes in AD.

M-L contacts have become of interest in more acute neuronal injuries, such as in seizures, as mitochondrial calcium overload contributes to neuronal dysfunction (Kovac et al, [2017](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR63 "Kovac S, Dinkova Kostova AT, Herrmann AM, Melzer N, Meuth SG, Gorji A (2017) Metabolic and homeostatic changes in seizures and acquired epilepsy-mitochondria, calcium dynamics and reactive oxygen species. Int J Mol Sci 18:1935")). It was found that during seizures, M-L contacts had an increased duration, which was alleviated by overexpression of TBC1D15 and preventing calcium overload in the mitochondria, and restored mitochondrial function. This suggests that TBC1D15 also has a neuroprotective effect.

Charcot–Marie–Tooth disease (CMT) encompasses genetically heterogeneous peripheral neuropathies, several forms of which arise from disrupted mitochondria–lysosome contact-site machinery. In CMT type 2 (CMT2), mutations in RAB7—which governs the tethering and untethering of mitochondria–lysosome contacts (Meggouh et al, [2006](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR85 "Meggouh F, Bienfait HME, Weterman MAJ, de Visser M, Baas F (2006) Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease due to a de novo mutation of the RAB7 gene. Neurology 67:1476–1478"))—impair GTP hydrolysis and increase the length of mitochondrial contacts (Wong et al, [2018](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR138 "Wong YC, Ysselstein D, Krainc D (2018) Mitochondria-lysosome contacts regulate mitochondrial fission via RAB7 GTP hydrolysis. Nature 554:382–386"), [2019](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR137 "Wong YC, Peng W, Krainc D (2019) Lysosomal regulation of inter-mitochondrial contact fate and motility in Charcot-Marie-Tooth Type 2. Dev Cell 50:339–354.e4")). CMT can also result from mutations in GDAP1, which, as discussed above, promotes a distinct class of mitochondria–lysosome contacts through its interaction with LAMP1 (Cantarero et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR16 "Cantarero L, Juárez-Escoto E, Civera-Tregón A, Rodríguez-Sanz M, Roldán M, Benítez R, Hoenicka J, Palau F (2021) Mitochondria-lysosome membrane contacts are defective in GDAP1-related Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease. Hum Mol Genet 29:3589–3605")). GDAP1 mutations cause both the axonal form CMT2K, the most common GDAP1-related presentation, and the demyelinating form CMT4A; M-L contacts are defective in GDAP1-related CMT (Cantarero et al, [2021](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR16 "Cantarero L, Juárez-Escoto E, Civera-Tregón A, Rodríguez-Sanz M, Roldán M, Benítez R, Hoenicka J, Palau F (2021) Mitochondria-lysosome membrane contacts are defective in GDAP1-related Charcot-Marie-Tooth disease. Hum Mol Genet 29:3589–3605")), indicating that disrupted contact-site regulation is a shared mechanism across genetically distinct CMT subtypes.

## Mitochondria–lysosome crosstalk in aging

Aging is characterized by the progressive decline of cellular homeostasis, ultimately leading to impaired tissue function and increased susceptibility to disease. The updated Hallmarks of Aging framework recognizes twelve interconnected hallmarks, including mitochondrial dysfunction, disabled macroautophagy, deregulated nutrient sensing, chronic inflammation, and altered intercellular communication (López-Otín et al, [2023](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR74 "López-Otín C, Blasco MA, Partridge L, Serrano M, Kroemer G (2023) Hallmarks of aging: an expanding universe. Cell 186:243–278")). Rather than acting independently, these hallmarks form an integrated network in which dysfunction in one process propagates across multiple cellular systems. In this context, mitochondria–lysosome communication has emerged as a critical interface integrating metabolic adaptation, organelle quality control, and stress responses. Consequently, progressive deterioration of this communication network is likely to contribute simultaneously to several hallmarks of aging.

One of the manifestations of cellular aging is the decline in organelle quality control. Although the role of mitophagy in aging has been extensively reviewed (Picca and Ferrucci, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR106 "Picca A, Ferrucci L (2026) Mitochondrial quality control in human ageing and longevity. Nat Metab 8:1464–1482")), recent studies indicate that the underlying defect extends beyond impaired mitochondrial degradation. Suppression of basal mitophagy is sufficient to drive multiple features of cellular aging, including mitochondrial dysfunction and senescence (Kelly et al, [2024](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR56 "Kelly G, Kataura T, Panek J, Ma G, Salmonowicz H, Davis A, Kendall H, Brookes C, Ayine-Tora DM, Banks P et al (2024) Suppressed basal mitophagy drives cellular aging phenotypes that can be reversed by a p62-targeting small molecule. Dev Cell 59:1924–1939.e7")), whereas enhancing neuronal mitophagy delays systemic aging and improves organismal health in _Drosophila_ (Schmid et al, [2022](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR115 "Schmid ET, Pyo J-H, Walker DW (2022) Neuronal induction of BNIP3-mediated mitophagy slows systemic aging in Drosophila. Nat Aging 2:494–507")). Progressive lysosomal dysfunction further limits mitochondrial turnover, establishing a feed-forward cycle in which defective lysosomal function promotes mitochondrial damage, while dysfunctional mitochondria exacerbate lysosomal impairment (Deus et al, [2020](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR34 "Deus CM, Yambire KF, Oliveira PJ, Raimundo N (2020) Mitochondria-lysosome crosstalk: from physiology to neurodegeneration. Trends Mol Med 26:71–88")). This process seems a well-conserved intrinsic property of eukaryotic cells, as the increase in vacuolar pH in yeast drives mitochondrial malfunction and cellular aging (Hughes and Gottschling, [2012](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR50 "Hughes AL, Gottschling DE (2012) An early age increase in vacuolar pH limits mitochondrial function and lifespan in yeast. Nature 492:261–265")). Thus, impaired organelle turnover should be viewed as one manifestation of a broader deterioration of mitochondria–lysosome communication rather than an isolated defect in degradative capacity.

The signaling pathways that coordinate mitochondrial and lysosomal function are likewise progressively remodeled during aging. Nutrient-sensing pathways centered on AMPK, mTORC1, and TFEB exhibit age-dependent dysregulation, reducing the adaptive capacity of both organelles. Consistent with this concept, age-associated decline of the glucose-responsive transcription factor MondoA impairs autophagy, disrupts mitochondrial homeostasis and promotes cellular senescence (Yamamoto-Imoto et al, [2022](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR139 "Yamamoto-Imoto H, Minami S, Shioda T, Yamashita Y, Sakai S, Maeda S, Yamamoto T, Oki S, Takashima M, Yamamuro T et al (2022) Age-associated decline of MondoA drives cellular senescence through impaired autophagy and mitochondrial homeostasis. Cell Rep 38:110444")). These findings suggest that aging is accompanied by a gradual loss of coordinated signaling between mitochondria and lysosomes, ultimately compromising cellular resilience to metabolic and environmental stress.

Emerging evidence further indicates that aging alters the physical interface between mitochondria and lysosomes. Using both yeast and mammalian cells, Tian et al ([2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR125 "Tian Z, Chen R, Fang G, Qiu K, Wu W, Shao X, Liu D, Que H, Wang X, Gao J et al (2026) Mitochondria acidify lysosomes through membrane contacts. Cell Rep 45:117112")) demonstrated that proton transfer from mitochondria to lysosomes at membrane contact sites contributes directly to lysosomal acidification, and that this mechanism becomes progressively impaired during cellular senescence owing to a reduction in M-L contacts (Tian et al, [2026](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1038/s44321-026-00556-9#ref-CR125 "Tian Z, Chen R, Fang G, Qiu K, Wu W, Shao X, Liu D, Que H, Wang X, Gao J et al (2026) Mitochondria acidify lysosomes through membrane contacts. Cell Rep 45:117112")). These observations reveal that membrane contact sites not only coordinate ion and metabolite exchange but also actively sustain lysosomal function, identifying their age-dependent decline as another mechanism linking mitochondrial dysfunction to impaired proteostasis.

Collectively, these studies support a shift in perspective from considering mitochondria and lysosomes as independent contributors to aging toward viewing their communication network as a central determinant of cellular resilience. Rather than representing separate aging mechanisms, defects in mitochondrial quality control, nutrient sensing, lysosomal function, and membrane contact sites converge through the progressive deterioration of mitochondria–lysosome communication. Defining how these communication pathways are remodeled across tissues during physiological aging, how adaptive communication transitions into maladaptive signaling, and whether inter-organelle communication can be selectively restored represent some of the most pressing challenges for the field. Addressing these questions may reveal strategies capable of simultaneously targeting multiple hallmarks of aging, positioning mitochondria–lysosome communication as a promising therapeutic axis for promoting healthy longevity.

## Toward a unified framework of mitochondria–lysosome communication

The past decade has fundamentally reshaped our understanding of the relationship between mitochondria and lysosomes. Once viewed primarily through the lens of mitophagy, their interplay is now recognized as a dynamic communication network that integrates metabolism, nutrient sensing, membrane trafficking, organelle quality control, and cellular adaptation. Rather than acting as isolated entities, mitochondria and lysosomes continuously exchange information through membrane contact sites, selective vesicular trafficking, and signaling pathways that coordinate organelle behavior across multiple spatial and temporal scales. This emerging view suggests that cellular homeostasis is maintained not by the autonomous function of individual organelles, but by the coordinated operation of an interconnected organelle network.

A major challenge for the field is to move beyond cataloging the molecular components involved in these interactions toward understanding the principles that govern inter-organelle communication. What molecular signals are exchanged between mitochondria and lysosomes, and how are they encoded, transmitted, and interpreted? How do different modes of communication—including contact sites, lipid transfer, Ca 2+ signaling, reactive oxygen species, metabolites, and MDVs—integrate into coherent cellular responses? Equally important will be to understand how these communication pathways differ across tissues, developmental stages, and disease contexts, and how acute adaptive responses transition into the chronic maladaptive signaling that characterizes aging and human disease. Finally, how does the crosstalk between mitochondria and lysosomes impact other organelles, and is it impacted by them?

Addressing these questions will require the development of technologies capable of measuring inter-organelle communication with the same spatial and temporal resolution that is now routinely achieved for individual organelles. Integrating quantitative imaging, organelle-specific omics, metabolic tracing, and functional perturbation approaches will make it possible to define the molecular logic that underlies organelle communication in living cells and tissues. Such efforts will also help distinguish causal communication pathways from secondary consequences of organelle dysfunction.

The convergence of cell biology, metabolism, biophysics, and systems biology places the field at an exciting inflection point. As the molecular language of mitochondria–lysosome communication begins to be deciphered, it is becoming increasingly clear that the communication network itself, rather than either organelle in isolation, represents the fundamental unit of regulation.

The next frontier is no longer to identify additional components of mitochondria–lysosome crosstalk, but to decipher the molecular language through which these organelles encode, transmit, and interpret information. Achieving this goal will establish a unified framework for mitochondria–lysosome communication and create new opportunities to therapeutically restore cellular homeostasis by targeting the communication network itself rather than either organelle in isolation.

### Pending issues

1.   1.
**Deciphering the molecular language of inter-organelle communication**.

What constitutes information in organelle communication, and how is it encoded, transmitted, and interpreted? A major challenge will be to understand how ions, lipids, metabolites, reactive oxygen species, proteins, and vesicular cargo are integrated with membrane contact sites and transcriptional responses to generate coordinated cellular decisions.

2.   2.
**Defining the spatial and temporal organization of organelle communication networks**.

Inter-organelle communication is highly dynamic and context-dependent, yet most studies capture individual interactions at isolated time points. How do communication networks reorganize across seconds, hours, and years, and across distinct subcellular regions? Understanding how acute adaptive communication transitions into chronic maladaptive states will be particularly important for explaining aging and disease.

3.   3.
**Moving from pairwise interactions to the organelle interactome**.

Mitochondria–lysosome communication does not occur in isolation: mitochondria and lysosomes simultaneously interact with the ER, peroxisomes, Golgi, lipid droplets, and other compartments, including at multi-organelle contact sites. The field must move beyond studying organelle pairs toward determining how information flows through an interconnected cellular organelle network and identifying the regulatory nodes that coordinate it.

4.   4.
**Establishing the physiological and therapeutic relevance of inter-organelle communication in vivo**.

Much of our mechanistic understanding derives from cultured cells, whereas communication networks are likely to differ profoundly among cell types, tissues, developmental stages, and metabolic states. Defining these tissue-specific communication programs in vivo—and determining whether dysfunctional communication can be selectively restored without perturbing physiological interactions—will be essential for translating organelle biology into therapeutic strategies.

## Glossary

**AMP-activated protein kinase (AMPK)**
A cellular energy sensor activated when energy availability is low. AMPK coordinates metabolic adaptation and can promote mitochondrial and lysosomal biogenesis and function.

**Inter-organelle communication (organelle crosstalk)**
The exchange of molecules, signals, or information between cellular organelles that allows their activities to be coordinated rather than functioning independently.

**Membrane contact sites (MCSs)**
Regions where the membranes of two organelles come into close proximity without fusing. These interfaces facilitate the exchange of molecules and information, including ions, lipids, and metabolic signals.

**Mitochondria-derived vesicles (MDVs)**
Small membrane-bound carriers that bud from mitochondria and selectively transport mitochondrial components to other cellular compartments, including lysosomes and peroxisomes. MDVs can contribute to mitochondrial quality control without requiring degradation of the entire mitochondrion.

**Mitochondria–lysosome crosstalk**
The collective processes through which mitochondria and lysosomes influence each other’s behavior and function. These include degradation of mitochondrial material, vesicular trafficking, membrane contact sites, and bidirectional signaling.

**mTOR complex 1 (mTORC1)**
A nutrient-sensitive signaling complex activated at the lysosomal surface that coordinates cell growth and metabolism with nutrient availability.

**TRPML1 (transient receptor potential mucolipin 1)**
A calcium-permeable ion channel in the lysosomal membrane, encoded by MCOLN1. Release of lysosomal calcium through TRPML1 regulates lysosomal function and can influence mitochondrial physiology.
